Barr-Exact Categories and Soft Sheaf Representations Thanks: Research supported by the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Skłodowska-Curie grant agreement No 837724 and by the Italian Ministry of University and Research through the PRIN project n. 20173WKCM5 Theory and applications of resource sensitive logics .
Marco Abbadini, Luca Reggio

Abstract
. It has long been known that a key ingredient for a sheaf representation of a universal algebra A A consists in a distributive lattice of commuting congruences on A A . The sheaf representations of universal algebras (over stably compact spaces) that arise in this manner have been recently characterised by Gehrke and van Gool (J. Pure Appl. Algebra, 2018), who identified the central role of the notion of softness . In this paper, we extend the scope of this theory by replacing varieties of algebras with Barr-exact categories, thus encompassing a number of “non-algebraic” examples. Our approach is based on the notion of 𝒦 \mathcal{K} -sheaf : intuitively, whereas sheaves are defined on open subsets, 𝒦 \mathcal{K} -sheaves are defined on compact ones. Throughout, we consider sheaves on complete lattices rather than spaces; this allows us to obtain point-free versions of sheaf representations whereby spaces are replaced with frames. These results are used to construct sheaf representations for the dual of the category of compact ordered spaces, and to recover Banaschewski and Vermeulen’s point-free sheaf representation of commutative Gelfand rings (Quaest. Math., 2011).



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 54B40; Secondary 18E08, 16S60, 18F20.

1.
Introduction

Sheaf representations of universal algebras have been investigated since the 1970s, see e.g.
[
12
,
13
,
16
,
30
,
46
]
, inspired by several results for rings and modules obtained in the 1960s, see e.g.
[
14
,
15
,
26
,
41
]
.
In particular it was observed that, for a universal algebra
A
, any distributive lattice of pairwise commuting congruences on
A
induces a sheaf representation of
A
, i.e. a sheaf whose algebra of global sections is isomorphic to
A

[
46
]
.
The sheaf representations over
stably compact
spaces
[
33
]
arising in this way were characterised by Gehrke and van Gool
[
21
]
, who recognised the key role of the notion of softness—which originated with Godement’s treatment of homological algebra
[
24
]
. A sheaf over a space
X
is
soft
if, for all compact saturated
1
1

1

A subset of a topological space is
saturated
if it is an intersection of open sets. In a
T_{1}
space, all subsets are saturated.
subsets
K\subseteq X
, every (continuous) local section over
K
can be extended to a (continuous) global section. In
[
21
]
, a bijection was established between isomorphism classes of soft sheaf representations of an algebra
A
over a stably compact space
X
, and frame homomorphisms from the
co-compact dual
frame of
X
to a frame of pairwise commuting congruences on
A
.

A sheaf representation of an algebra can be regarded as a generalisation of a representation in terms of continuous functions. For example, the Gelfand–Naimark theorem
[
22
]
states that for every commutative unital
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
A
there is an isomorphism

A\cong\mathrm{C}(\Max{A},\mathbb{C})

where
\mathrm{C}(\Max{A},\mathbb{C})
is the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra of all continuous complex-valued functions on the maximal spectrum
\Max{A}
of
A
. More precisely, it provides a characterisation of the image of the embedding

A\rightarrowtail\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\Max{A}}{A/{\mathfrak{m}}},\ \ \ a\mapsto(a/{\mathfrak{m}})_{\mathfrak{m}\in\Max{A}}

where each
A/{\mathfrak{m}}
is an isomorphic copy of
\mathbb{C}
.
Sheaf representations extend these ideas to a wider class of rings—and, more generally, universal algebras—by allowing the factors in the direct product to vary “continuously”.

Thus, disregarding the topological constraint of continuity of global sections, sheaf representations of universal algebras are akin to embeddings into direct products. From this standpoint, the softness condition for sheaves is related to a basic concept of universal algebra, namely that of
subdirect representation
. In fact, any sheaf representation
F
of a universal algebra
A
on a space
X
induces an embedding

\nu\colon A\rightarrowtail\prod_{x\in X}{F_{x}}

where
F_{x}
is the stalk of
F
at
x
. If
F
is soft then
\nu
is a
subdirect embedding
, i.e. for all
y\in X
the composition of
\nu
with the product projection
\prod_{x\in X}{F_{x}}\twoheadrightarrow F_{y}
is surjective.

In this article, we generalise Gehrke and van Gool’s characterisation of soft sheaf representations by replacing varieties of finitary algebras—in which sheaves take values—with any
Barr-exact
category.
Barr-exact categories, introduced in
[
6
]
, are a non-additive generalisation of Abelian categories. Examples of Barr-exact categories include most “algebraic-like” categories such as varieties of (possibly infinitary) algebras, any topos, the category of compact Hausdorff spaces and its opposite category.

This allows us to construct soft sheaf representations of all objects in the category
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
opposite to the category of compact ordered spaces and continuous monotone maps. This category can be regarded as an extension of the variety
\mathsf{DLat}
of bounded distributive lattices, in the sense that
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
admits a full subcategory equivalent to
\mathsf{DLat}
(this follows from Priestley duality between bounded distributive lattices and totally order-disconnected compact ordered spaces
[
42
]
). The category
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
is Barr-exact and even equivalent to a variety of algebras, but not a finitary one.

We hasten to point out that, while the intended application of our results concerns Barr-exact categories, we develop the theory more generally for
regular
categories
[
6
]
. Examples of regular categories that are not Barr-exact include quasi-varieties of (possibly infinitary) algebras and the category of Boolean (i.e., compact, Hausdorff and zero-dimensional) spaces and continuous maps.

Barring some examples and applications, we always work with sheaves over complete lattices. The usual notion of a sheaf on a space
X
is recovered by considering sheaves over the frame of opens of
X
, but this approach accommodates also point-free sheaf representations by taking sheaves on possibly non-spatial frames.
For example, we illustrate how Banaschewski and Vermeulen’s sheaf representation of commutative Gelfand rings on compact regular frames
[
5
]
can be recovered as a special case of our results.

Our approach crucially relies on the notion of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf
. In the spatial setting, a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf can be thought of as a “sheaf defined on compact saturated subsets” (or equivalently, in the case of
T_{1}
spaces, on compact subsets) instead of open ones. The concept of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf essentially originates with Leray’s pioneering work on sheaves and sheaf cohomology
[
34
]
(see also
[
17
, Chapter IV, §7.B]
) and has been fruitfully employed by Lurie in the theory of
\infty
-categories, cf.
[
35
, §7.3.4]
.

Outline

In
section

2
, we recall the basic definitions and properties pertaining to the theory of regular and Barr-exact categories.
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves over a complete lattice, with values in a regular category, are introduced in
section

3
. In
section

4
the notion of softness for
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves is defined and, for any object of a regular category, an isomorphism is established between a category of soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations and a category of monotone maps preserving finite infima and arbitrary suprema (
Theorem

4.7
).

section

5
contains some background material on domains and algebraic lattices that is needed in
section

6
to show that, under appropriate assumptions,
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves are equivalent to ordinary sheaves (
Theorem

6.21
). This equivalence is then extended to (soft) sheaf representations and leads to our main result,
Theorem

6.31
. Finally, in
section

7
, these results are applied to study soft sheaf representations of commutative Gelfand rings and of objects in the dual of the category of compact ordered spaces.

Notation and terminology

A poset
P
is
directed
if each of its finite subsets has an upper bound; equivalently, if it is non-empty and any two of its elements admit an upper bound. A subset
D\subseteq P
is said to be directed if it satisfies the previous condition with respect to the order induced by
P
. The order-dual notion is that of
codirected
subset.

We often identify a preordered set
S
with the (small) category whose set of objects is
S
and such that, for all
s,t\in S
, there is exactly one morphism
s\to t
if
s\leq t
, and there is no morphism otherwise. More generally, categories in which there is at most one morphism between any two objects are identified with (possibly large) preorders.

Arrows
\rightarrowtail
and
\twoheadrightarrow
denote, respectively, monomorphisms and regular epimorphisms. The terminal object in a category, if it exists, is denoted by
\mathbf{1}
.

Whenever
\mathsf{C}
is a category,
\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
denotes the
opposite category
obtained by reversing the direction of arrows in
\mathsf{C}
. This applies in particular when
\mathsf{C}
is a poset, e.g. the complete lattice
\Omega(X)
of open subsets of a topological space
X
, ordered by set-theoretic inclusion.

We write
\Omega
for the contravariant functor from the category of topological spaces and continuous maps, to the category of frames and their homomorphisms, that sends a continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
to the frame homomorphism
\Omega(f)\coloneqq f^{-1}\colon\Omega(Y)\to\Omega(X)
.

A
\mathsf{C}
-valued presheaf
on a poset
P
is a functor

F\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}.

If
p,q\in P
satisfy
p\leq q
, the image under
F
of the unique arrow
q\to p
in
P^{\mathrm{op}}
is denoted by
{F}_{{q},{p}}\colon F(q)\to F(p)
.

2.
Preliminaries on Regular Categories

We recall some basic definitions and facts concerning regular and Barr-exact categories.

2.1.
Subobjects and quotients

\mathsf{C}
and an object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
. The collection of all monomorphisms in
\mathsf{C}
with codomain
A
carries a natural preorder
\leq
defined as follows. Given monomorphisms
m\colon S\rightarrowtail A
and
n\colon T\rightarrowtail A

(Note that, if it exists, such an
l
is a monomorphism.) The symmetrization
\sim
of the preorder
\leq
can be characterised explicitly as follows:
m\sim n
if, and only if, there exists an isomorphism
l
such that
m=n\circ l
. A
subobject
of
A
is a
\sim
-equivalence class of monomorphisms with codomain
A
, and the collection of all subobjects of
A
is denoted by
\Sub{A}
. The preorder
\leq
induces a partial order on
\Sub{A}
, which we denote again by
\leq
. As
A
may admit a proper class of subobjects, in general
\Sub{A}
is a large poset.

In the same fashion, we can define the (large) poset of quotients of an object
A
. To this end, we introduce a preorder on the class of all regular epimorphisms with domain
A
. We use again the symbol
\leq
for this preorder; it will be clear from the context to which (pre)order we are referring. Given regular epimorphisms
f\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
and
g\colon A\twoheadrightarrow C
, set

f\leq g\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \exists\,h.\ f=h\circ g\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox to76.81pt{\vbox to47.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.40446pt\lower-23.15279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(Note that, if it exists, such an
h
is an epimorphism, but need not be a regular epimorphism unless
\mathsf{C}
admits (regular epi, mono) factorisations.)
As before, the symmetrization
\sim
of the preorder
\leq
can be characterised explicitly by:
f\sim g
if, and only if, there exists an isomorphism
h
such that
f=h\circ g
. A
quotient
of
A
is a
\sim
-equivalence class of regular epimorphisms with domain
A
, and the collection of all quotients of
A
is denoted by
\Quo{A}
. The preorder
\leq
induces a partial order on
\Quo{A}
, that we shall denote again by
\leq
. Equivalently,
\Quo{A}
can be identified with the poset of
regular
subobjects of
A
in the opposite category
\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

We will often work with the category

\RegEpi{A}

whose objects are regular epimorphisms in
\mathsf{C}
with domain
A
. For any two regular epimorphisms
f\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
and
g\colon A\twoheadrightarrow C
, an arrow
f\to g
in
\RegEpi{A}
is an arrow
h\colon B\to C
in
\mathsf{C}
such that
h\circ f=g
. In other words,
\RegEpi{A}
is a full subcategory of the coslice category
A/{\mathsf{C}}
. Note that, since each object of
\RegEpi{A}
is an epimorphism in
\mathsf{C}
, the category
\RegEpi{A}
is a (large) preorder.
The poset reflection of
\RegEpi{A}
coincides with the
opposite
of
\Quo{A}
.

2.2.
Regular categories

Definition 2.1
.

A category
\mathsf{C}
is
regular
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

\mathsf{C}
has finite limits.

(ii)

\mathsf{C}
has (regular epi, mono) factorisations, i.e. every arrow
f
in
\mathsf{C}
can be written as
f=m\circ e
where
e
is a regular epimorphism and
m
a monomorphism.

(iii)

Regular epimorphisms in
\mathsf{C}
are stable under pullbacks along any morphism.

Note that, because every regular epimorphism is a strong epimorphism, for every commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

there exists a (unique)
diagonal filler
, i.e. an arrow
B\to C
making the ensuing triangles commute. Although we shall not need this fact, let us mention that in any regular category the strong and regular epimorphisms coincide; see e.g.
[
6
, Proposition 1.4, p. 129]
.

Some useful consequences of the axioms for a regular category are collected in the following lemma; these properties rely on the fact that the (regular epi, mono) factorisation system in a regular category is orthogonal, proper and stable. Cf. e.g.
[
19
]
or
[
20
, §1.5]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The following statements hold in any regular category
\mathsf{C}
:

(a)

The composition of regular epimorphisms is again a regular epimorphism.

(b)

If
f\circ g
is a regular epimorphism, then so is
f
.

(c)

Any pullback square consisting entirely of regular epimorphisms is also a pushout square.

Proof.

For
items

(a)
and
(b)
, see
[
6
, Propositions 1.10 and 1.11, p. 133]
.
Item

(c)
follows from (the dual of) the main result of
[
43
]
(cf. also
[
20
, §1.565]
or
[
11
, Remark 5.3]
).
∎

Whenever
A
is an object of a regular category
\mathsf{C}
, the preorder
\RegEpi{A}
admits finite infima. Just observe that an infimum of a finite set of regular epimorphisms

\{f_{i}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B_{i}\mid i\in I\}

is given by the (regular epi, mono) factorisation of the induced morphism
A\to\prod_{i\in I}{B_{i}}
.

\RegEpi{A}
has also a minimum, namely the identity of
A
; in fact,
A\twoheadrightarrow B
is a minimum in
\RegEpi{A}
if, and only if, it is an isomorphism. However, non-empty suprema in
\RegEpi{A}
may fail to exist. For the next lemma, recall that the pushout of a regular epimorphism—if it exists—is again a regular epimorphism.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a regular category,
A
an object of
\mathsf{C}
, and
f\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
and
g\colon A\twoheadrightarrow C
regular epimorphisms. Then
f
and
g
admit a supremum in
\RegEpi{A}
if, and only if, they admit a pushout in
\mathsf{C}
.

(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

In that case, the composite
\eta_{1}\circ f=\eta_{2}\circ g
is a supremum of
f
and
g
.

Proof.

Suppose that the diagram in
eq.

1
is a pushout.
By
item

(a)
in
Lemma

2.2
,
h\coloneqq\eta_{1}\circ f
(
=\eta_{2}\circ g
) is an element of
\RegEpi{A}
that is above
f
and
g
, and the universal property of the pushout readily implies that
h
is a supremum of
f
and
g
.

For the converse direction, assume
f
and
g
admit a supremum
h\colon A\twoheadrightarrow H
in
\RegEpi{A}
. In particular, as
h
is above
f
and
g
, there exist morphisms
\eta_{1}\colon B\to H
and
\eta_{2}\colon C\to H
such that
\eta_{1}\circ f=h=\eta_{2}\circ g
. Hence the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{2}}

Note that
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
are regular epimorphisms by
item

(b)
in
Lemma

2.2
. We claim that the square above is a pushout. Consider morphisms
\sigma_{1}\colon B\to J
and
\sigma_{2}\colon C\to J
such that
\sigma_{1}\circ f=\sigma_{2}\circ g
, and let
(e,m)
be the (regular epi, mono) factorisation of
\sigma_{1}\circ f=\sigma_{2}\circ g
. Then there are diagonal fillers
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
as displayed below.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Hence,
e
is above
f
and
g
in
\RegEpi{A}
. Since
h
is a supremum of
f
and
g
, there exists a morphism
\xi\colon H\to K
satisfying
e=\xi\circ h
. Because
f
and
g
are epimorphisms, we see that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

The composite morphism
m\circ\xi\colon H\to J
then satisfies
(m\circ\xi)\circ\eta_{1}=\sigma_{1}
and
(m\circ\xi)\circ\eta_{2}=\sigma_{2}
. As
\eta_{1}
(or, equivalently,
\eta_{2}
) is an epimorphism, it follows that
m\circ\xi
is the unique arrow with this property.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

With regards to the previous lemma, a closely related fact was proved by Burgess and Caicedo, cf.
[
10
, Proposition 10]
.

The following is a consequence of
Lemma

2.3
and the preceding discussion:

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a regular category admitting pushouts. For any object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
, its poset of quotients
\Quo{A}
is a (possibly large) bounded lattice.

2.3.
Barr-exact categories

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a regular category and let
A
be an object of
\mathsf{C}
. A subobject
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle\colon R\rightarrowtail A\times A
is called a
relation
on
A
, and it is an
equivalence relation
provided it satisfies the following three properties:

Reflexivity.
There exists an arrow
d\colon A\to R
in
\mathsf{C}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle}

Symmetry.
There exists an arrow
s\colon R\to R
in
\mathsf{C}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle p_{2},p_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle}

Transitivity.
For any pullback diagram in
\mathsf{C}
as on the left-hand side below, there exists an arrow
t\colon P\to R
such that the rightmost diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle p_{1}\circ\pi_{1},p_{2}\circ\pi_{2}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle}

Example 2.6
.

If
\mathsf{C}
is a variety of algebras then an equivalence relation on an algebra
A
, in the sense above, coincides with the usual notion of
congruence
.

With any arrow
f\colon A\to B
in
\mathsf{C}
we can associate a relation on
A
, known as the
kernel pair
of
f
. This is obtained by taking the pullback of
f
along itself:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The kernel pair
\ker{f}\coloneqq\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle
is a relation on
A
. Just recall that the pullback of
f
along itself can equivalently be computed as the equaliser of the pair of parallel arrows
f\circ\pi_{1},f\circ\pi_{2}\colon A\times A\rightrightarrows B
, where
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\colon A\times A\rightrightarrows A
are the product projections. In fact,
\ker{f}
is always an equivalence relation on
A
.

The collection
\Equiv{A}
of all equivalence relations on
A
carries a natural partial order, induced by the order of
\Sub{(A\times A)}
. The following is an immediate consequence of
[
6
, Propositions 5.3 and 5.4, pp. 156–157]
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a regular category and let
A
be an object of
\mathsf{C}
. Then

\ker\colon(\Quo{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Equiv{A}

is an order embedding between (large) posets.

The equivalence relations in the image of the map
\ker\colon(\Quo{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Equiv{A}
are called
effective
. In general, there may be equivalence relations on
A
that are not effective. This leads us to the following notion, first introduced in
[
6
]
.

Definition 2.8
.

A
Barr-exact category
is a regular category in which every equivalence relation is effective.

Example 2.9
.

Any variety of (possibly infinitary) algebras, with morphisms all the homomorphisms, is a Barr-exact category. The category of compact Hausdorff spaces and continuous maps is Barr-exact and, more generally, so is any category that is monadic over the category of sets. Any (elementary) topos is in particular a Barr-exact category.

Remark 2.10
.

In view of
Lemma

2.7
, a regular category is Barr-exact precisely when the map
\ker\colon(\Quo{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Equiv{A}
is an order isomorphism for all objects
A
.

Remark 2.11
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{C}
is a regular category admitting coequalisers of equivalence relations. Then, for every object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
, there is a monotone map

\coeq\colon\Equiv{A}\to(\Quo{A})^{\mathrm{op}}

sending an equivalence relation
\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle\colon R\rightarrowtail A\times A
to the coequaliser of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. The map
\coeq
is left inverse to
\ker\colon(\Quo{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Equiv{A}
. Hence,
(\Quo{A})^{\mathrm{op}}
is a retract of
\Equiv{A}
in the category of (large) posets and monotone maps.

3.
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves

As mentioned in the Introduction,
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves can be regarded as “sheaves defined on compact saturated subsets”. To make this intuition more precise, consider a sheaf of sets on a topological space
X
. That is, a presheaf

F\colon\Omega(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}

satisfying the
patch property
: for every set
\{U_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subseteq\Omega(X)
of open subsets of
X
and every tuple
(s_{i})_{i\in I}\in\prod_{i\in I}{F(U_{i})}
, if for all
i,j\in I
we have
{F}_{{U_{i}},{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}}(s_{i})={F}_{{U_{j}},{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}}(s_{j})
, then there exists a unique
s\in F(\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i})
such that
{F}_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}},{U_{j}}}(s)=s_{j}
for all
j\in I
.

Sheaves on
X
can be also described as
étale spaces
over
X
, i.e. local homeomorphisms

p\colon E\to X.

In fact, any étale space induces a sheaf
\Omega(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
that sends
U\in\Omega(X)
to the set of
local sections
of
p
over
U
, i.e. the continuous maps
s\colon U\to E
such that
p\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{U}
. The functorial action is given by restricting local sections to open subsets of their domains. Conversely, a sheaf
F
induces an étale space
p\colon E_{F}\to X
where
E_{F}
is obtained by “gluing together” the
stalks
of
F
, and the map
p
contracts the stalk of
F
at a point
x\in X
to
x
. These assignments yield an equivalence between the category of sheaves of sets over
X
and the category of étale spaces over
X
. See e.g.
[
37
, Corollary II.6.3]
.

If
\mathsf{V}
is any variety of finitary algebras, we can consider the categories of internal
\mathsf{V}
-algebras in the toposes of
\mathsf{Set}
-valued sheaves over
X
and of étale spaces over
X
, respectively. This yields an equivalence between
sheaves of
\mathsf{V}
-algebras
over
X
(that is, presheaves
\Omega(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{V}
satisfying the patch property) and
étale spaces of
\mathsf{V}
-algebras
over
X
; cf. e.g.
[
37
, §II.7]
.

Now, let
F\colon\Omega(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
be a sheaf with corresponding étale space
p\colon E\to X
. An advantage of the latter formulation is that we can consider sections of
p
over arbitrary subsets of
X
, not just the open ones. Thus, we can associate to any compact saturated subset
K\subseteq X
the set
G(K)
of local sections of
p
over
K
. This induces a presheaf
G
over the poset of compact saturated subsets of
X
ordered by inclusion. If
X
is nice enough (e.g., it is locally compact), the sheaf
F
can be recovered from
G
via the isomorphism

F(U)\cong\lim_{K\subseteq U}{G(K)}

where
K
ranges over the compact saturated sets contained in
U
. See e.g.
[
21
, Lemma 3.4]
. The notion of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf introduced in
Definition

3.3
below captures precisely the properties of the presheaf
G
, as will become clear in
section

6
.

Throughout this section, we fix an arbitrary regular category
\mathsf{C}
, an object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
, and a complete lattice
P
.
The latter can be thought of as the lattice of closed subsets of a compact Hausdorff space or, more generally, the lattice of compact saturated subsets of a
stably compact
space (see
Example

5.12
for a definition).

The following notion, along with the functor
\gamma_{*}
defined in
eq.

2
below, will play a central role in the remainder of the paper.

Definition 3.1
.

The
canonical representation
of
A
is the functor

\gamma\colon\RegEpi{A}\to\mathsf{C}

sending a regular epimorphism
A\twoheadrightarrow B
to its codomain (i.e.,
\gamma
is the restriction of the codomain functor
A/{\mathsf{C}}\to\mathsf{C}
).

Remark 3.2
.

The name “canonical representation” stems from the observation that, under certain conditions, the poset reflection of
\RegEpi{A}
can be thought of as the opposite of the poset of compact saturated subsets of a space
X
, and
\gamma(k)
as the set of local sections over
k
of a sheaf over
X
whose object of global sections is isomorphic to
A
.

The functor category
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\RegEpi{A}]
can be identified with the large preorder of monotone maps
P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A}
, with respect to the pointwise preorder. The canonical representation of
A
induces a “direct image” functor
\gamma_{*}
given by post-composing with
\gamma
:

(2)

\begin{aligned} \gamma_{*}\colon[P^{\mathrm{op}},\RegEpi{A}]&\to[P^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]\\
H&\mapsto\gamma\circ H\end{aligned}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \hbox to109pt{\vbox to49.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 54.50029pt\lower-24.18056pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

A morphism
\alpha\colon H\Rightarrow J
in
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\RegEpi{A}]
is sent by
\gamma_{*}
to the horizontal composition of natural transformations
\mathrm{id}_{\gamma}\alpha\colon\gamma_{*}H\Rightarrow\gamma_{*}J
.

In this section we shall see that order-theoretic properties of the monotone map
H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A}
correspond to certain sheaf-like properties—made precise in the following definition—of the associated presheaf
\gamma_{*}H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
.

Definition 3.3
.

A
\mathsf{C}
-valued
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf
over
P
is a functor
F\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
satisfying the following properties:

(K1)

F(\bot)
is a subterminal object of
\mathsf{C}
, i.e. the unique arrow
F(\bot)\to\mathbf{1}
is monic.

(K2)

For all
p,q\in P
, the following is a pullback square in
\mathsf{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p\vee q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p\wedge q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{p\vee q},{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{p\vee q},{q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{p},{p\wedge q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{q},{p\wedge q}}}

(K3)

F
preserves directed colimits. I.e., for all codirected subsets
D\subseteq P
, the cocone
({F}_{{p},{\bigwedge D}}\colon F(p)\to F(\bigwedge D))_{p\in D}
is a colimit of the restriction of
F
to
D
.

We let
\KSh(P,\mathsf{C})
denote the full subcategory of
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
defined by the
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves.

Remark 3.4
.

The definition of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf given above is a slight variant of the homonymous notion introduced by Lurie
[
35
, Definition 7.3.4.1]
. In
op. cit.
,
\mathsf{C}
is an
\infty
-category
and
P
is the lattice of compact subsets of a locally compact Hausdorff space.
Note that Lurie requires
F(\bot)
to be a terminal object of
\mathsf{C}
, whereas we relax this condition by replacing “terminal” with “subterminal”. We do this so that
Corollary

4.8
below (which generalises the main result of
[
21
]
for varieties of finitary algebras) holds for all objects of
\mathsf{C}
, including those objects
A
such that the unique morphism
A\to\mathbf{1}
is not a regular epimorphism. E.g., if
\mathsf{C}
is the variety of semigroups and
A
is the empty semigroup, i.e. the initial object of
\mathsf{C}
, the unique morphism
A\to\mathbf{1}
is not a regular epimorphism.

We shall also consider the following variant of condition
(K2)
:

(K4)

For all
p,q\in P
, the following is a pushout square in
\mathsf{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p\vee q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p\wedge q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{p\vee q},{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{p\vee q},{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{p},{p\wedge q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{q},{p\wedge q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A}
be a monotone map.
The following statements hold:

(a)

H
preserves the infimum of the empty set
2
2

2

That is,
H
sends the greatest element of
P^{\mathrm{op}}
(equivalently, the least element
\bot
of
P
) to a maximum in
\RegEpi{A}
. Similarly for the following items.
if and only if
\gamma_{*}H
satisfies
(K1)
.

(b)

H
preserves binary infima if and only if, for all
p,q\in P
, the following mediating morphism is monic:

\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)\to\gamma_{*}H(p)\times\gamma_{*}H(q).

(c)

H
preserves binary suprema if and only if
\gamma_{*}H
satisfies
(K4)
.

(d)

H
preserves directed suprema if and only if
\gamma_{*}H
satisfies
(K3)
.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary finite set
\{p_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subseteq P
. We claim that
H(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}})
is an infimum of
\{H(p_{i})\mid i\in I\}
if, and only if, the induced mediating morphism

\gamma_{*}H\bigg(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}}\bigg)\to\prod_{i\in I}{\gamma_{*}H(p_{i})}

is monic.
Items

(a)
and
(b)
then follow at once by letting
\{p_{i}\mid i\in I\}
be the empty set and any two-element set, respectively.
Recall that an infimum
t\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
of
\{H(p_{i})\mid i\in I\}
is obtained by taking the (regular epi, mono) factorisation of the arrow
A\to\prod_{i\in I}{\gamma_{*}H(p_{i})}
whose composition with the
i
th projection is
H(p_{i})
. Thus,
H(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}})
is an infimum of
\{H(p_{i})\mid i\in I\}
precisely when it is above
t
. Consider the following commutative square.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i\in I}{\gamma_{*}H(p_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

If the bottom horizontal arrow is monic, there is a diagonal filler
B\to\gamma_{*}H(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}})
, showing that
H(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}})
is above
t
. Conversely, if
H(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}})
is above
t
there is
h\colon B\to\gamma_{*}H(\bigvee_{i\in I}{p_{i}})
making the upper triangle commute, and such an
h
is necessarily a regular epimorphism. Using the fact that the outer square commutes and
t
is an epimorphism, we see that the lower triangle must also commute. It follows that
h
is also monic, hence an isomorphism, and so the bottom horizontal arrow is monic.

For
item

(c)
, note that
H
preserves binary suprema if and only if, for all
p,q\in P
,
H(p\wedge q)
is a supremum of
H(p)
and
H(q)
. In turn, by
Lemma

2.3
, this is equivalent to saying that the left-hand diagram below is a pushout in
\mathsf{C}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\wedge q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{p\wedge q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{q},{p\wedge q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p\vee q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{p}}}

Both
H(p)
and
H(q)
factor through the regular epimorphism

H(p\vee q)\colon A\twoheadrightarrow\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q),

as depicted in the rightmost diagram above.
Hence the leftmost diagram above is a pushout precisely when
\gamma_{*}H
satisfies
(K4)
.

For
item

(d)
, let
D
be a codirected subset of
P
. We must show that
H(\bigwedge D)
is a supremum of
\{H(p)\mid p\in D\}
if, and only if, the cocone

(3)

({\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge D}}\colon\gamma_{*}H(p)\to\gamma_{*}H(\bigwedge D))_{p\in D}

is a colimit of the restriction of
\gamma_{*}H
to
D
.
Suppose the latter is a colimit cocone.
Clearly,
H(\bigwedge D)
is above
H(p)
for all
p\in D
, so assume that
f\colon A\twoheadrightarrow B
is an element of
\RegEpi{A}
that is above
H(p)
for all
p\in D
. That is, for each
p\in D
there is
g_{p}\colon\gamma_{*}H(p)\to B
such that
g_{p}\circ H(p)=f
. Then
(g_{p}\colon\gamma_{*}H(p)\to B)_{p\in D}
is a compatible cocone over the diagram given by the restriction of
\gamma_{*}H
to
D
. Just observe that, for all
p,q\in D
such that
p\leq q
,

g_{p}\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{q},{p}}\circ H(q)=g_{p}\circ H(p)=f=g_{q}\circ H(q)

and so
g_{p}\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{q},{p}}=g_{q}
because
H(q)
is an epimorphism.
Hence there is a unique arrow
j\colon\gamma_{*}H(\bigwedge D)\to B
satisfying
j\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge D}}=g_{p}
for all
p\in D
. We then see that
f
is above
H(\bigwedge D)
because, if
p
is an arbitrary element of
D
,

j\circ H(\bigwedge D)=j\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge D}}\circ H(p)=g_{p}\circ H(p)=f.

Therefore,
H(\bigwedge D)
is a supremum of
\{H(p)\mid p\in D\}
.

Conversely, suppose that
H(\bigwedge D)
is a supremum of
\{H(p)\mid p\in D\}
and consider a compatible cocone
(h_{p}\colon\gamma_{*}H(p)\to B)_{p\in D}
over the diagram given by the restriction of
\gamma_{*}H
to
D
. Fix an arbitrary
p\in D
and take the (regular epi, mono) factorisation of
h_{p}\circ H(p)
, as depicted in the following diagram.

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

This yields
f\in\RegEpi{A}
. Note that, because
D
is codirected,
f
does not depend on the choice of
p
. To see this, pick another
q\in D
. As
D
is codirected, there is
r\in D
that is below
p
and
q
. So,

h_{r}\circ H(r)=h_{r}\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{r}}\circ H(p)=h_{p}\circ H(p)

and, by a similar reasoning,
h_{r}\circ H(r)=h_{q}\circ H(q)
. Thus,
h_{p}\circ H(p)=h_{q}\circ H(q)
. Now, observe that the square in
eq.

4
admits a diagonal filler
\gamma_{*}H(p)\to C
. Because
p\in D
was chosen arbitrarily, it follows that
f
is above
H(p)
for all
p\in D
. Since
H(\bigwedge D)
is a supremum of
\{H(p)\mid p\in D\}
, there is
j\colon\gamma_{*}H(\bigwedge D)\to C
such that
j\circ H(\bigwedge D)=f
. The composite
m\circ j\colon\gamma_{*}H(\bigwedge D)\to B
satisfies

m\circ j\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge D}}\circ H(p)=m\circ j\circ H(\bigwedge{D})=m\circ f=h_{p}\circ H(p)

and so
m\circ j\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge D}}=h(p)
because
H(p)
is an epimorphism. In other words,
m\circ j
is a morphism from the cocone in
eq.

3
to the cocone
(h_{p}\colon\gamma_{*}H(p)\to B)_{p\in D}
. Finally, observe that
m\circ j
is the unique such morphism, for if
n\colon\gamma_{*}H(\bigwedge D)\to B
is another morphism of cocones then, for all
p\in D
,

n\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge{D}}}=h_{p}=m\circ j\circ{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge{D}}}

entails
n=m\circ j
because
{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge{D}}}
is an epimorphism (just note that
{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{\bigwedge{D}}}\circ H(p)=H(\bigwedge D)
). Therefore, the cocone in
eq.

3
is a colimit of the restriction of
\gamma_{*}H
to
D
.
∎

Given relations

\langle r_{1},r_{2}\rangle\colon R\rightarrowtail A\times A\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \langle s_{1},s_{2}\rangle\colon S\rightarrowtail A\times A

on
A
, we can define their composition
R\circ S
as follows. Consider the following equaliser diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\times S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{2}\circ\pi_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}\circ\pi_{S}}

Then the (regular epi, mono) factorisation of the morphism

\langle r_{1}\circ\pi_{R}\circ u,s_{2}\circ\pi_{S}\circ u\rangle\colon U\to A\times A

yields the composite relation
U\twoheadrightarrow R\circ S\rightarrowtail A\times A
. See e.g.
[
20
, §1.56]
.

A classical result of universal algebra states that, for any two congruences
\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}
on an algebra, the composite
\vartheta_{1}\circ\vartheta_{2}
is a congruence if, and only if,
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
commute (i.e.
\vartheta_{1}\circ\vartheta_{2}=\vartheta_{2}\circ\vartheta_{1}
).
In our setting, congruences correspond to effective equivalence relations.
If the composition of two effective equivalence relations
\ker{f}
and
\ker{g}
is an effective equivalence relation,
\ker{f}
and
\ker{g}
commute (i.e.
\ker{f}\circ\ker{g}=\ker{g}\circ\ker{f}
). Conversely, if
\ker{f}
and
\ker{g}
commute, their composition
\ker{f}\circ\ker{g}
is an equivalence relation; however,
\ker{f}\circ\ker{g}
need not be effective. This leads us to the following notion:

Definition 3.6
.

Let
f,g\in\RegEpi{A}
. We say that
f
and
g

ker-commute
if the relations
\ker{f}
and
\ker{g}
commute and their composition is an effective equivalence relation.

Remark 3.7
.

If
\mathsf{C}
is Barr-exact, two regular epimorphisms
f,g\in\RegEpi{A}
ker-commute precisely when the relations
\ker{f}
and
\ker{g}
commute.
In fact, Barr-exact categories coincide with the regular categories in which the composition of any pair of commuting effective equivalence relations is an effective equivalence relation
[
10
, Theorem 17]
.

To state the next lemma, we recall the following terminology from
[
8
]
(cf. also
[
11
]
). In any regular category, a commutative diagram of regular epimorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is a
regular pushout
if the unique mediating morphism from
B
to the pullback of
h
along
i
is a regular epimorphism. (This nomenclature is justified by virtue of the fact that any regular pushout in a regular category is a pushout
[
8
, p. 118]
.)
For a proof of the next result, cf.
[
10
, Lemma 9 and Propositions 10 and 12]
.

Lemma 3.8
.

The following statements are equivalent for all
f,g\in\RegEpi{A}
:

(1)

f
and
g
ker-commute.

(2)

f
and
g
admit a supremum
h
in
\RegEpi{A}
and
\ker{f}\circ\ker{g}=\ker{h}
.

(3)

The pushout in
\mathsf{C}
of
f
along
g
exists and is a regular pushout.

Remark 3.9
.

With regards to the previous result, a related fact was proved by Fay in
[
18
]
, motivated by an earlier unpublished version of
[
10
]
whose main results were announced in
[
9
]
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A}
be a monotone map.
The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

H
preserves binary infima and binary suprema, and its image consists of pairwise ker-commuting elements.

(2)

\gamma_{*}H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
satisfies
(K2)
.

Proof.

Assume that
1
holds and consider the following commutative diagram, where
\chi
is the unique mediating morphism induced by the universal property of the pullback (recall that every arrow in the image of
\gamma_{*}H
is a regular epimorphism).

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\wedge q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{p\wedge q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{q},{p\wedge q}}}

The composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)\times\gamma_{*}H(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle p_{1},p_{2}\rangle}

coincides with the pairing of
{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{p}}
and
{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{q}}
. As
H
preserves binary infima, the latter pairing is a monomorphism by
item

(b)
in
Proposition

3.5
, and so
\chi
is monic.
On the other hand, because
H
preserves binary suprema, the outer diagram in
eq.

5
is a pushout by
item

(c)
in
Proposition

3.5
. Thus, the diagram obtained by precomposing with the (regular) epimorphism
H(p\vee q)\colon A\twoheadrightarrow\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)
is also a pushout:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\wedge q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{p\wedge q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{q},{p\wedge q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

The unique arrow
A\to P
induced by the universal property of the pullback coincides with
\chi\circ H(p\vee q)
and is a regular epimorphism by
Lemma

3.8
, since
H(p)
and
H(q)
ker-commute. It follows from
item

(b)
in
Lemma

2.2
that
\chi
is a regular epimorphism, hence an isomorphism. Thus the outer diagram in
eq.

5
is a pullback, i.e.
\gamma_{*}H
satisfies
(K2)
.

Conversely, suppose that
2
holds and fix arbitrary elements
p,q\in P
.
As
\gamma_{*}H
satisfies
(K2)
, the following is a pullback square.

(6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\wedge q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{p\wedge q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{q},{p\wedge q}}}

The pairing of
{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{p}}
and
{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{q}}
is then an equaliser of the arrows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)\times\gamma_{*}H(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\wedge q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{p\wedge q}}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)\times\gamma_{*}H(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\wedge q),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{q},{p\wedge q}}}

where the first morphisms in the two compositions are the appropriate product projections.
In particular, the mediating morphism

\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)\to\gamma_{*}H(p)\times\gamma_{*}H(q)

given by the pairing of
{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{p}}
and
{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{q}}
is monic. It follows from
item

(b)
in
Proposition

3.5
that
H
preserves binary infima.
Moreover, the square in
eq.

6
is a pushout by
item

(c)
in
Lemma

2.2
, and so an application of
item

(c)
in
Proposition

3.5
shows that
H
preserves binary suprema.
Finally, in order to prove that
H(p)
and
H(q)
ker-commute, consider the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p\wedge q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(p\lor q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p\vee q},{q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{p},{p\wedge q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{q},{p\wedge q}}}

The inner square is a pushout, and the morphism
H(p\vee q)\colon A\twoheadrightarrow\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)
is an epimorphism (in fact, a regular epimorphism).
Therefore, the outer square is a pushout.
Since the inner square is a pullback and the morphism
H(p\vee q)\colon A\twoheadrightarrow\gamma_{*}H(p\vee q)
is a regular epimorphism, we see that the outer square is a regular pushout.
Therefore,
H(p)
and
H(q)
ker-commute by
Lemma

3.8
.
∎

We thus obtain a characterisation of those monotone maps
H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A}
such that the presheaf
\gamma_{*}H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
is a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf.

Theorem 3.11
.

Consider a monotone map
H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A}
.
The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

H
preserves finite infima and non-empty suprema, and its image consists of pairwise ker-commuting elements.

(2)

\gamma_{*}H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
is a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf.

Proof.

In view of
items

(a)
and
(d)
in
Proposition

3.5
, combined with
Lemma

3.10
,
\gamma_{*}H
satisfies
(K1)
–
(K3)
if, and only if,
H
preserves finite infima and non-empty suprema and its image consists of pairwise ker-commuting elements. Just observe that
H
preserves non-empty suprema precisely when it preserves binary and directed suprema.
∎

Corollary 3.12
.

Consider a monotone map
H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A}
.
The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

H
preserves finite infima and arbitrary suprema, and its image consists of pairwise ker-commuting elements.

(2)

\gamma_{*}H\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
is a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf and
H(\top)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Theorem

3.11
. Just observe that
H
preserves the supremum of the empty set if, and only if,

H(\top)\colon A\to\gamma_{*}H(\top)

is a minimum in
\RegEpi{A}
, i.e. an isomorphism in
\mathsf{C}
.
∎

4.
Soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf Representations

As in the previous section, we fix an arbitrary regular category
\mathsf{C}
, an object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
, and a complete lattice
P
.

To start with, we introduce the concept of softness for presheaves. We note that, by
item

(b)
in
Lemma

2.2
, all arrows in the image of the canonical representation
{\gamma\colon\RegEpi{A}\to\mathsf{C}}
are regular epimorphisms.
Therefore, this property is inherited by all presheaves of the form
\gamma_{*}H
with
H\in[P^{\mathrm{op}},\RegEpi{A}]
. The following observation is straightforward:

Lemma 4.1
.

The following statements are equivalent for any presheaf
F\in[P^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
:

(1)

{F}_{{\top},{p}}\colon F(\top)\to F(p)
is a regular epimorphism for all
p\in P
.

(2)

{F}_{{q},{p}}\colon F(q)\to F(p)
is a regular epimorphism for all
p,q\in P
with
p\leq q
.

Proof.

Clearly,
2
implies
1
. Conversely, suppose
1
holds and let
p,q\in P
satisfy
{p\leq q}
.
By functoriality of
F
we have
{F}_{{q},{p}}\circ{F}_{{\top},{q}}={F}_{{\top},{p}}
, which is a regular epimorphism. It follows from
item

(b)
in
Lemma

2.2
that
{F}_{{q},{p}}
is a regular epimorphism.
∎

Definition 4.2
.

A presheaf
P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
is said to be
soft
if it satisfies either of the equivalent conditions in
Lemma

4.1
.

Remark 4.3
.

By
item

(c)
in
Lemma

2.2
, any soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf satisfies
(K4)
.

Next, we look at those
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves
P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
which allow us to recover
A
, up to isomorphism, as the object of global sections.

Definition 4.4
.

A
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representation
of
A
over
P
is a pair
(F,\varphi)
where
F\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
is a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf and
\varphi\colon A\to F(\top)
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{C}
. If
F
is soft, we call
(F,\varphi)
a
soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representation
of
A
over
P
.

Denote by

\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})

the category of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations of
A
over
P
. The objects of
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
are
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations
(F,\varphi)
of
A
over
P
, and a morphism
(F,\varphi)\to(G,\psi)
is a natural transformation
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G
satisfying
\alpha_{\top}\circ\varphi=\psi
. The full subcategory of
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
defined by the
soft

\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations is denoted by

\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C}).

Lemma 4.5
.

The following statements hold:

(a)

For any two objects
(F,\varphi),(G,\psi)\in\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
, if
(F,\varphi)
is a soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representation, there is at most one morphism
(F,\varphi)\to(G,\psi)
in
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
.

(b)

\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
is a (large) preorder.

Proof.

For
item

(a)
, consider any two arrows
\alpha,\beta\colon(F,\varphi)\rightrightarrows(G,\psi)
in
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
such that
F
is soft. We claim that
\alpha_{p}=\beta_{p}
for all
p\in P
, and so
\alpha=\beta
.
For every
p\in P
, we have

Naturality of
\alpha

\displaystyle\alpha_{p}\circ{F}_{{\top},{p}}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle={{G}_{{\top},{p}}}\circ\alpha_{\top}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle={{G}_{{\top},{p}}}\circ\psi

\displaystyle={{G}_{{\top},{p}}}\circ\beta_{\top}\circ\varphi

Naturality of
\beta

\displaystyle=\beta_{p}\circ{{F}_{{\top},{p}}}\circ\varphi.

As
\varphi
is an isomorphism we get
\alpha_{p}\circ{{F}_{{\top},{p}}}=\beta_{p}\circ{{F}_{{\top},{p}}}
. Since
F
is soft,
{F}_{{\top},{p}}
is an epimorphism. Hence,
\alpha_{p}=\beta_{p}
.

Item

(b)
is an immediate consequence of
item

(a)
.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
(F,\varphi)\in\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
and define the monotone map

H_{F}\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A},\ \ p\mapsto(A\xrightarrow{\varphi}F(\top)\xrightarrow{{F}_{{\top},{p}}}F(p)).

The following statements hold:

(a)

\gamma_{*}H_{F}=F
.

(b)

H_{F}
preserves finite infima and arbitrary suprema, and its image consists of pairwise ker-commuting elements.

Proof.

For
item

(a)
, note that an arrow
p\to q
in
P
is sent by
\gamma_{*}H_{F}
to the unique arrow
h\colon F(q)\to F(p)
in
\mathsf{C}
such that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{F}(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{F}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

It follows that
h={{F}_{{q},{p}}}
because

{{F}_{{q},{p}}}\circ H_{F}(q)={{F}_{{q},{p}}}\circ{{F}_{{\top},{p}}}\circ\varphi={{F}_{{\top},{p}}}\circ\varphi=H_{F}(p),

and so
\gamma_{*}H_{F}=F
.

Item

(b)
is an immediate consequence of
item

(a)
combined with
Corollary

3.12
. Just observe that
H_{F}(\top)=\varphi
is an isomorphism.
∎

The following is our main result concerning soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations of objects of regular categories. Recall that
P
denotes an arbitrary complete lattice, and
A
an object of a regular category
\mathsf{C}
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
\mathsf{M}
be the (large) sub-preorder of
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\RegEpi{A}]
consisting of those maps that preserve finite infima and arbitrary suprema, and whose images consist of pairwise ker-commuting elements. The functor
\gamma_{*}\colon[P^{\mathrm{op}},\RegEpi{A}]\to[P^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
in
eq.

2
induces an isomorphism

\mathsf{M}\cong\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C}).

Proof.

Define the functor

\Xi\colon\mathsf{M}\to\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C}),\ \ H\mapsto(\gamma_{*}H,H(\top)).

An arrow
\alpha\colon H\Rightarrow J
in
\mathsf{M}
is sent to
\gamma_{*}\alpha
.
By
Corollary

3.12
, the presheaf
\gamma_{*}H
is a (soft)
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf and
H(\top)
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{C}
.
Thus,
(\gamma_{*}H,H(\top))
is a soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representation of
A
. If
\alpha\colon H\Rightarrow J
is an arrow in
\mathsf{M}
then
\alpha_{\top}\circ H(\top)=J(\top)
in
\mathsf{C}
, and so
(\gamma_{*}\alpha)_{\top}\circ H(\top)=J(\top)
. Therefore, the functor
\Xi
is well-defined.

In view of
item

(b)
in
Lemma

4.5
,
\Xi
is a functor between (large) preorders, i.e. a monotone map. We claim that
\Xi
is an isomorphism of categories, i.e. an order isomorphism. To show that
\Xi
is an order embedding, we must prove that there is an arrow
H\Rightarrow J
in
\mathsf{M}
whenever there is an arrow
\delta\colon(\gamma_{*}H,H(\top))\to(\gamma_{*}J,J(\top))
in
\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
. For all
p\in P
we have a commutative diagram as displayed below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}J(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}H(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{*}J(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{*}\delta)_{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}H}_{{\top},{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{*}J}_{{\top},{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{*}\delta)_{p}}

Just observe that the triangle commutes because
\delta
is a morphism of soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations, and the rectangle commutes by naturality of
\gamma_{*}\delta
. The composites

{\gamma_{*}H}_{{\top},{p}}\circ H(\top)\ \ \text{ and }\ \ {\gamma_{*}J}_{{\top},{p}}\circ J(\top)

coincide with
H(p)
and
J(p)
, respectively. This shows that
H(p)
is below
J(p)
for all
p\in P
, so there is an arrow
H\Rightarrow J
in
\mathsf{M}
.

Next, we show that
\Xi
is surjective, hence an order isomorphism. Fix an arbitrary
(F,\varphi)\in\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
and consider the monotone map
H_{F}\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\RegEpi{A}
defined in
Proposition

4.6
. Then
H_{F}\in\mathsf{M}
by
item

(b)
in
Proposition

4.6
, so we can consider its image
\Xi(H_{F})=(\gamma_{*}H_{F},H_{F}(\top))
. In view of
item

(a)
in
Proposition

4.6
we have
\gamma_{*}H_{F}=F
. Moreover
H_{F}(\top)=\varphi
, showing that
\Xi(H_{F})=(F,\varphi)
.
∎

The isomorphism of categories
\mathsf{M}\cong\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
in
Theorem

4.7
can be equivalently understood as an order isomorphism between (large) preorders, which in turn induces an order isomorphism between the corresponding poset reflections. We state this observation in the following corollary in the special case of Barr-exact categories, where quotients can be replaced with equivalence relations. Let us write
\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})\rrbracket
for the poset reflection of
\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
; the objects of
\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})\rrbracket
are isomorphism classes of soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations of
A
over
P
.

Corollary 4.8
.

Assume
\mathsf{C}
is Barr-exact.
Let
\mathsf{N}
be the (large) sub-poset of
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\Equiv{A}]
consisting of those maps that preserve finite infima and arbitrary suprema, and whose images consist of pairwise commuting equivalence relations. There is an order isomorphism

\mathsf{N}\cong\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})\rrbracket.

Proof.

It follows at once from
Theorem

4.7
that there is an order isomorphism

\llbracket\mathsf{M}\rrbracket\cong\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})\rrbracket

between the poset reflections of
\mathsf{M}
and
\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(P,\mathsf{C})
, respectively. So, it suffices to show that
\llbracket\mathsf{M}\rrbracket\cong\mathsf{N}
.
The poset reflection
\llbracket\RegEpi{A}\rrbracket
of
\RegEpi{A}
coincides with the opposite of
\Quo{A}
. If
\mathsf{C}
is Barr-exact, the map
\ker\colon(\Quo{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Equiv{A}
is an order isomorphism (see
Remark

2.10
), and so
\llbracket\RegEpi{A}\rrbracket
is isomorphic to
\Equiv{A}
. Thus, the poset reflection of
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\RegEpi{A}]
is isomorphic to
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\Equiv{A}]
. This isomorphism restricts to an isomorphism between
\llbracket\mathsf{M}\rrbracket
and
\mathsf{N}
because, under the isomorphism between
\llbracket\RegEpi{A}\rrbracket
and
\Equiv{A}
, the (isomorphism classes of) ker-commuting elements of
\RegEpi{A}
correspond to commuting elements of
\Equiv{A}
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

If we required that
F(\bot)
be a terminal object in the definition of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf, then
Corollary

4.8
(as well as
Theorem

4.7
) would no longer be true. Just observe that, if
\mathsf{C}=\mathsf{Set}
and
A=\emptyset
, then both
\Equiv{A}
and
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\Equiv(A)]
are one-element posets, and thus so is
\mathsf{N}
. By
Corollary

4.8
, there is exactly one (isomorphism class of) soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representation of
A
over
P
, given by the constant functor
F\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
defined by
F(p)=\emptyset
for all
p\in P
. But
F(\bot)=\emptyset
is a proper subterminal object in
\mathsf{Set}
.

Remark 4.10
.

Suppose that the category
\mathsf{C}
is
well-powered
, i.e.
\Sub{A}
is a set—as opposed to a proper class—for all objects
A
of
\mathsf{C}
. Then
\Equiv{A}
is a small poset, and the isomorphism in
Corollary

4.8
is an isomorphism between small posets. This is the case, for example, when
\mathsf{C}
is
\mathsf{Set}
or a variety of (possibly infinitary) algebras.

5.
Domains and Continuous Lattices

In this brief interlude, we shall recall some basic material concerning domains and continuous lattices that will be needed in
section

6
. For a more thorough treatment, the reader can consult e.g.
[
23
]
.

Let
P
be a poset. The
way-below
relation on
P
, denoted by
\ll
, is defined as follows: for all
x,y\in P
,
x\ll y
precisely when, for all directed subsets
D\subseteq P
admitting a supremum
\bigvee{D}
in
P
, if
y\leq\bigvee{D}
there is
d\in D
such that
x\leq d
.

Remark 5.1
.

If
P
is a complete lattice and
x,y\in P
, then
x\ll y
if and only if, for all subsets
Y\subseteq P
, whenever
y\leq\bigvee{Y}
there is a finite subset
X\subseteq Y
such that
x\leq\bigvee{X}
.

Example 5.2
.

Recall that a topological space is
locally compact
if each of its points admits a compact neighbourhood.
Let
X
be a locally compact space and consider its frame of opens
\Omega(X)
. For all
U,V\in\Omega(X)
,
U\ll V
if and only if there is a compact subset
C\subseteq X
such that
U\subseteq C\subseteq V
.

Definition 5.3
.

For any poset
P
, we shall say that:

•

P
is a
directed complete partially ordered set
(
dcpo
, for short) if every directed subset
D\subseteq P
has a supremum in
P
;

•

P
is
continuous
if, for all
x\in P
, the set

.

A dcpo that is continuous is called a
domain
.
A
continuous lattice
is a domain that is complete as a lattice.

Remark 5.4
.

Whenever the poset
P
has a least element
\bot
, we have
\bot\ll x
for all
x\in P
.
Moreover, for all
x,y,z\in P
such that the supremum
x\vee y
exists in
P
,
x\ll z
and
y\ll z
entail
x\vee y\ll z
, see e.g.
[
23
, Proposition I-1.2(iii)]
.
It follows that, whenever
P
has all finite suprema, the set

.

Example 5.5
.

For a locally compact space
X
, its frame of opens
\Omega(X)
is a continuous lattice. See e.g.
[
23
, p. 56]
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
U
be an upwards closed subset of a dcpo
P
. We say that
U
is
Scott-open
if, for all directed subsets
D\subseteq P
,
\bigvee{D}\in U
entails
D\cap U\neq\emptyset
.

Recall that a
filter
on a poset
P
is an upwards closed subset of
P
that is codirected (in particular, non-empty). Write

\mathrm{Filt}(P)

for the poset of filters on
P
, partially ordered by inclusion.
\mathrm{Filt}(P)
is a dcpo, and is a complete lattice whenever
P
has binary suprema and a top element. A filter on a dcpo that is Scott-open as an upwards closed subset is called a
Scott-open filter
. The set of all Scott-open filters on a dcpo
P
is denoted by

\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(P)

and regarded as a sub-poset of
\mathrm{Filt}(P)
. The poset
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(P)
is often referred to as the
Lawson dual
of
P
; it is always a dcpo, with directed suprema given by set-theoretic unions, and is a domain whenever
P
is a domain (see e.g.
[
23
, Theorem II-1.17]
). Further, if
P
admits finite infima,
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(P)
has finite infima given by set-theoretic intersections.

We now recall some basic properties of Scott-open filters on a domain. To this end, for any element
x
of a poset
P
, set
{\uparrow}\,x\coloneqq\{y\in P\mid x\leq y\}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
L
be a domain. The following statements hold:

(a)

For all
x,y\in L
,
y\ll x
precisely when there is
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
such that
x\in k\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y
.

(b)

For all
x\in L
and all
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
, if
x\in k
there is
y\ll x
such that
y\in k
.

(c)

For all
k,l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
,
l\ll k
precisely when there is
x\in k
such that
l\subseteq{\uparrow}\,x
.

(d)

For all
x\in L
and all
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
, if
x\in k
there is
l\ll k
such that
x\in l
.

(e)

For all
x\in L
, the set
\{k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\mid x\in k\}
is codirected.

Proof.

The left-to-right implication in
item

(a)
follows from
[
23
, Proposition I-3.3(i)]
, while the converse is a consequence of the definition of Scott-open filter.
For
items

(b)
and
(c)
, see e.g. Proposition II-1.10(i) and Theorem II-1.17(ii), respectively, in
[
23
]
.

For
item

(d)
, let
x\in L
and
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
satisfy
x\in k
. By
item

(b)
, there is
y\ll x
such that
y\in k
. Thus, in view of
item

(a)
, there exists
l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
such that
x\in l\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y
. Since
y\in k
and
l\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y
, by
item

(c)
we get
l\ll k
.

Finally, let us prove
item

(e)
. Let
S
be a finite subset of
\{k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\mid x\in k\}
. For each
l\in S
, pick
y_{l}\in l
such that
y_{l}\ll x
(existence is guaranteed by
item

(b)
). Since
L
is a domain, the set
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\downarrow$}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip 3.88885pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\hbox{\hskip 3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\hbox{$\textstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip 3.88885pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.3611pt}{\resizebox{566377}{407778}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\hbox{\hskip 2.36311pt\hskip-2.36311pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-2.36311pt\hskip-2.36311pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-2.36311pt\hskip 2.36311pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.97221pt}{\resizebox{552772}{291270}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4861pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox{\hskip 1.64737pt\hskip-1.64737pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-1.64737pt\hskip-1.64737pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-1.64737pt\hskip 1.64737pt}$}}}}}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\downarrow$}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip 3.88885pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\hbox{\hskip 3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\hbox{$\textstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip such that
y_{l}\leq y
for all
l\in S
. By
item

(a)
, there is
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
such that
x\in k\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y
. For every
l\in S
we have
k\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y_{l}\subseteq l
. Therefore,
k
is a lower bound of
S
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Items

(d)
and
(e)
in
Lemma

5.7
can be strengthened to the effect that, for every element
x
of a domain
L
, the set
\mathcal{U}(x)\coloneqq\{k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\mid x\in k\}
is a Scott-open filter on
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
. In fact, the Scott-open filters on
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
are precisely those of the form
\mathcal{U}(x)
, for
x\in L
, and the map
\mathcal{U}(-)\colon L\to\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L))
is an order isomorphism. These observations are at the base of the Lawson Duality Theorem of Domains
[
23
, Theorem IV-2.14]
. For a proof of the properties mentioned above, cf.
[
23
, §IV-2]
.

is immediate because
x\ll y\leq z
implies
x\ll z
. For the converse inclusion just recall that, in a continuous poset,
x\ll\bigvee{D}
implies the existence of
d\in D
such that
x\ll d
, see e.g.
[
23
, Theorem I-1.9]
.
∎

We record for future reference the following fact, which is known as
Wilker’s condition
when
L=\Omega(X)
for a locally compact space
X
(cf.
[
31
]
or
[
21
, Lemma 2.3]
).

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
L
be a continuous lattice.
For all
x,y\in L
and
l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
such that
x\vee y\in l
, there are
k,k^{\prime}\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
such that
x\in k
,
y\in k^{\prime}
and
k\wedge k^{\prime}\subseteq l
.

such that

x^{\prime}\vee y^{\prime}=\bigvee I\in l.

In view of
item

(a)
in
Lemma

5.7
, there are
k,k^{\prime}\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
such that

x\in k\subseteq{\uparrow}\,x^{\prime}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ y\in k^{\prime}\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y^{\prime}.

The Scott-open filters
k,k^{\prime}
then satisfy the desired property. Just observe that, if
z\in L
belongs to both
k
and
k^{\prime}
, then
x^{\prime}\leq z
and
y^{\prime}\leq z
entail
x^{\prime}\vee y^{\prime}\leq z
. As
x^{\prime}\vee y^{\prime}\in l
and the latter is upwards closed, we get
z\in l
.
∎

Given a topological space
X
, let
\mathcal{K}(X)
denote the poset of compact saturated subsets of
X
, ordered by inclusion (recall that a set is
saturated
if it is an intersection of opens).
The Hofmann–Mislove theorem states that, for any
sober
space
X
(i.e., one in which every irreducible closed subset is the closure of a unique point), the monotone map

\Phi\colon\mathcal{K}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(\Omega(X)),\ \ \Phi(K)\coloneqq\{U\in\Omega(X)\mid K\subseteq U\}

is an order isomorphism. Its inverse sends a Scott-open filter of open sets to its intersection. See e.g.
[
23
, Theorem II-1.20]
.

Even when
P
is a continuous lattice,
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(P)
need not be complete (equivalently, a continuous lattice) because it may fail to admit binary suprema. Thus, we shall now focus on a class of continuous lattices whose posets of Scott-open filters are complete.

Definition 5.11
.

The way-below relation
\ll
in a continuous lattice
L
with top element
\top
is said to be
multiplicative
provided that
\top\ll\top
and, for all
x,y,z\in L
,
x\ll y
and
x\ll z
entail
x\ll y\wedge z
.

A continuous lattice whose way-below relation is multiplicative is called a
stably continuous lattice
. If
L
is a stably continuous lattice then so is
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
, see e.g.
[
28
, §VII.2.12]
. In this case, the supremum of Scott-open filters
U,V
is given by
{\uparrow}\,\{u\wedge v\mid u\in U,\,v\in V\}
.

Example 5.12
.

For a
T_{0}
space
X
, its frame of opens
\Omega(X)
is a stably continuous lattice if and only if
X
is
stably compact
, i.e.
T_{0}
, compact, locally compact, coherent
3
3

3

A topological space is
coherent
if the intersection of any two compact saturated subsets is compact.
and sober. See e.g.
[
23
, Proposition VI-7.3]
.
Stably compact spaces generalise both compact Hausdorff spaces and spectral spaces, and are tightly related to compact ordered spaces, cf.
section

7.1
.

6.
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves and
\Omega
-sheaves

Throughout this section, we fix an arbitrary domain
L
. There are order embeddings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Filt}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

where
\lambda
sends
x\in L
to the principal filter
{\uparrow}\,x
, and
\kappa
is the inclusion of Scott-open filters on
L
into filters on
L
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be the union of the images of
\lambda
and
\kappa
. We regard
\mathcal{F}
as a sub-poset of
\mathrm{Filt}(L)
and, with a slight abuse of notation, write again
\lambda\colon L^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{F}
and
\kappa\colon\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{F}
for the obvious co-restrictions. The latter order embeddings will be regarded as functors between small (posetal) categories.

In the first part of this section we only assume that
\mathsf{C}
is a
bicomplete
(i.e., complete and cocomplete) category. The functors
\lambda,\kappa
induce “restriction” functors
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{*}}

and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{*}}

given by precomposing with
\lambda
and
\kappa
, respectively. As
L^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
are small categories and
\mathsf{C}
is bicomplete, any functor
G
in
[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
admits a left Kan extension
\Lan_{\lambda}G
along
\lambda
, and any functor
F
in
[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
admits a right Kan extension
\Ran_{\kappa}F
along
\kappa
. These Kan extensions are computed pointwise and determine two adjunctions as displayed below (see e.g.
[
36
, §X.3]
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{*}}
\top
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lan_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{*}}
\top
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ran_{\kappa}}

Let
\eta,\varepsilon
be the unit and counit, respectively, of the adjunction
\Lan_{\lambda}\dashv\lambda^{*}
, and
\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\varepsilon}
the unit and counit, respectively, of the adjunction
\kappa^{*}\dashv\Ran_{\kappa}
.

As
\lambda
and
\kappa
are order embeddings (hence, fully faithful), these adjunctions fix all objects of
[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
and
[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
, respectively. This is the content of the next lemma, which holds more generally for Kan extensions along fully faithful functors, cf.
[
32
, Proposition 4.23]
.

Lemma 6.1
.

The following statements hold:

(a)

The unit
\eta
of the adjunction
\Lan_{\lambda}\dashv\lambda^{*}
is a natural isomorphism, i.e. for all
G\in[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
,
\eta_{G}\colon G\cong\lambda^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G
.

(b)

The counit
\tilde{\varepsilon}
of the adjunction
\kappa^{*}\dashv\Ran_{\kappa}
is a natural isomorphism, i.e. for all
F\in[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
,
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{F}\colon\kappa^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F\cong F
.

Remark 6.2
.

Recall that, given any adjunction, the right adjoint is fully faithful precisely when the counit is a natural isomorphism. A dual statement holds for left adjoint functors and units. Therefore,
Lemma

6.1
amounts to saying that the functors
\Ran_{\kappa}
and
\Lan_{\lambda}
are fully faithful.

Composing the two adjunctions above, we obtain the following pair of adjoint functors:

(7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}}
\top
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}}

In
Proposition

6.9
below we shall characterise the objects that are fixed by the unit and counit of this adjunction, thus obtaining an equivalence between the full subcategories defined by the fixed objects.
By further restricting this equivalence, in
Theorem

6.21
we will relate the notion of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf to the usual notion of sheaf.

Remark 6.3
.

We provide explicit descriptions of the adjoint functors in
eq.

7
. These are easy consequences of the formulas for pointwise Kan extensions, cf.
[
36
, §X.5]
.
For all
G\colon L^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
and
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
,

(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G)(k)

is the directed colimit in
\mathsf{C}
of the restriction of
G
to
k
(regarded as a subset of
L
).
If
k,l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
are such that
k\subseteq l
, the corresponding arrow

(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G)(k)\to(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G)(l)

is the mediating morphism induced by the universal (colimit) property of
(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G)(k)
.

On the other hand, for all
F\colon\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\to\mathsf{C}
and
x\in L
,

(\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F)(x)

is the codirected limit in
\mathsf{C}
of the restriction of
F
to
\{k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\mid x\in k\}
. Note that the latter set is codirected by
item

(e)
in
Lemma

5.7
.
Given
x,y\in L
with
x\leq y
, the corresponding morphism

(\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F)(y)\to(\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F)(x)

is the one induced by the universal (limit) property of
(\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F)(x)
.

Remark 6.4
.

Assume that
X
is a sober space. By the Hofmann–Mislove theorem, we have an isomorphism
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(\Omega(X))\cong\mathcal{K}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}
. Hence, for any sheaf of sets
G\colon\Omega(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
over
X
,
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G
can be identified with a presheaf
\mathcal{K}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
. For any point
x\in X
, the up-set
{\uparrow}\,{x}
of
x
in the specialization order of
X
is a compact saturated subset of
X
. In view of
Remark

6.3
, the value of
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G
at
{\uparrow}\,{x}
is

(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G)({\uparrow}\,{x})\cong\colim_{{\uparrow}\,{x}\subseteq U\in\Omega(X)}{G(U)}\cong\colim_{x\in U\in\Omega(X)}{G(U)},

which is precisely the stalk of
G
at
x
. A similar remark applies when
\mathsf{Set}
is replaced with any variety of algebras.

Lemma 6.5
.

Consider a functor
F\colon L\to\mathsf{C}
. The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

F
preserves directed colimits.

(2)

For all
x\in L
, the cocone
({F}_{{y},{x}}\colon F(y)\to F(x))_{y\in{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\downarrow$}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\displaystyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip 3.88885pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\hbox{\hskip 3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\hbox{$\textstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip-3.88885pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-3.88885pt\hskip 3.88885pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.3611pt}{\resizebox{566377}{407778}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\hbox{\hskip 2.36311pt\hskip-2.36311pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-2.36311pt\hskip-2.36311pt\raisebox{2.08334pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-2.36311pt\hskip 2.36311pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.97221pt}{\resizebox{552772}{291270}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.4861pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox{\hskip is an immediate consequence of

Conversely, suppose that
2
holds and fix an arbitrary directed set
D\subseteq L
. We must show that the cocone

({F}_{{y},{\bigvee D}}\colon F(y)\to F\big(\bigvee D\big))_{y\in D}

is a colimit of the restriction of
F
to
D
. Let
(\varphi_{y}\colon F(y)\to A)_{y\in D}
be a compatible cocone over the restriction of

to
D
.

does not depend on the choice of

.

We claim that
\eta
is the unique arrow such that
\varphi_{y}=\eta\circ{F}_{{y},{\bigvee{D}}}
for all
y\in D
, thus showing that
({F}_{{y},{\bigvee D}}\colon F(y)\to F(\bigvee D))_{y\in D}
is a colimit cocone.

y\in D
.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
