,

\varphi_{y}\circ{F}_{{z},{y}}=\varphi_{y_{z}}\circ{F}_{{z},{y_{z}}}=\psi_{z}=\eta\circ{F}_{{z},{\bigvee{D}}}.

Clearly,
\eta
is unique with respect to this property.
∎

It is useful to record the dual version of the previous lemma:

Lemma 6.6
.

Consider a functor
F\colon L^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
. The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

F
preserves codirected limits.

(2)

.

Proof.

This follows by applying
Lemma

6.5
to the functor
F^{\mathrm{op}}\colon L\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
∎

Now, denote by

\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]

the full subcategory of
[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
defined by the functors preserving codirected limits, and by

\omega\text{-}\colim[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]

the full subcategory of
[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
defined by the functors preserving directed colimits.

Lemma 6.7
.

The following statements hold:

(a)

For all
G\in[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
, the functor
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G
belongs to
\omega\text{-}\colim[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
.

(b)

For all
F\in[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
, the functor
\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F
belongs to
\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
.

2.86267pt\hskip-2.86267pt\hbox{$\textstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-2.86267pt\hskip-2.86267pt\raisebox{1.04167pt}{\hbox{$\textstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-2.86267pt\hskip 2.86267pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.68054pt}{\resizebox{263305}{203888}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.47638pt}{$\scriptstyle\hbox{\hskip 1.0986pt\hskip-1.0986pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-1.0986pt\hskip-1.0986pt\raisebox{1.04167pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-1.0986pt\hskip 1.0986pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.4861pt}{\resizebox{221364}{145634}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.24304pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox{\hskip 0.65971pt\hskip-0.65971pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\downarrow$}\hskip-0.65971pt\hskip-0.65971pt\raisebox{1.04167pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\downarrow$}}\hskip-0.65971pt\hskip 0.65971pt}$}}}}}}}}\,k}
.
Therefore, using
Remark

6.3
twice, we have

(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G)(k)\cong\colim_{x\in k}G(x)\cong\colim_{l\ll k,\,x\in l}G(x)\cong\colim_{l\ll k}\colim_{x\in l}G(x)\cong\colim_{l\ll k}(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G)(l).

This slick proof does not show that the cocone above is a colimit cocone, although this could be deduced by checking how the colimit cocones are modified under the chain of isomorphisms. We now give a detailed proof that follows precisely this intuition.

To start with, observe that
\kappa^{*}
is left adjoint and so it preserves colimits. Thus, it is enough to show that the cocone

is a limit of the restriction of
\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F
to
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip .
By
item

(b)
in
Lemma

5.7
, we have

\{l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\mid x\in l\}=\{l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\mid\exists y\in L\text{ such that }y\ll x\text{ and }y\in l\}.

Using
Remark

6.3
twice, we get

(\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F)(x)\cong\lim_{x\in l}F(l)=\lim_{y\ll x}\lim_{y\in l}F(l)=\lim_{y\ll x}(\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}F)(y),

where
l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
.
Reasoning along the same lines as before, it is possible to give a direct proof that the cone above is a limit cone; we leave the details to the reader.
∎

As a consequence of
Lemma

6.7
, every object of
[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
that is fixed by the unit of the adjunction in
eq.

7
belongs to
\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
, and every object of
[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
that is fixed by the counit belongs to
\omega\text{-}\colim[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
.
The next lemma will imply that the converse implications hold as well.

Recall that
\tilde{\eta}
is the unit of the adjunction
\kappa^{*}\dashv\Ran_{\kappa}
and
\varepsilon
is the counit of
{\Lan_{\lambda}\dashv\lambda^{*}}
.
Intuitively, for all functors
F\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathsf{C}
,
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism precisely when, if we first restrict
F
along
\kappa^{*}
and then extend along
\Ran_{\kappa}
, we recover
F
up to a natural isomorphism. A similar remark applies to the case where
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism.

Lemma 6.8
.

The following conditions are equivalent for all functors
F\in[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]
:

(1)

\kappa^{*}F\in\omega\text{-}\colim[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
and
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism.

(2)

\lambda^{*}F\in\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
and
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let us prove that
1
implies
2
. Fix an arbitrary functor
F\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathsf{C}
such that
\kappa^{*}F
preserves directed colimits and
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism.
By
item

(b)
in
Lemma

6.7
applied to
\kappa^{*}F
, we have that
\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}\kappa^{*}F
preserves codirected limits. In view of the isomorphism
\lambda^{*}(\tilde{\eta}_{F})\colon\lambda^{*}F\cong\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}\kappa^{*}F
, we get
\lambda^{*}F\in\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
.

Next, we show that
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism.
That is, for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}
,

(\varepsilon_{F})_{\varphi}\colon(\Lan_{\lambda}\lambda^{*}F)(\varphi)\to F(\varphi)

is an isomorphism.
By the formula for pointwise left Kan extensions (cf.
[
36
, §X.5]
),
(\varepsilon_{F})_{\varphi}
can be identified with the unique mediating morphism

\colim_{{\uparrow}\,x\subseteq\varphi}F({\uparrow}\,x)\to F(\varphi)

for
x\in L
. If
\varphi
belongs to the image of
\lambda
(i.e.,
\varphi
is a principal filter) then this mediating morphism is clearly an isomorphism. Hence it remains to prove that, for all
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
, the following arrow is an isomorphism:

\colim_{x\in k}F({\uparrow}\,x)\to F(k).

Let
\{\psi_{x}\colon F({\uparrow}\,x)\to A\mid x\in k\}
be a compatible cocone in
\mathsf{C}
over the restriction of
F
to
\{{\uparrow}\,x\mid x\in k\}
. By
item

(c)
in
Lemma

5.7
, whenever two Scott-open filters
k
and
k^{\prime}
satisfy
k^{\prime}\ll k
, there is
x\in k
such that
k^{\prime}\subseteq{\uparrow}\,x
. Hence, for every
k^{\prime}\in{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 3.88885pt}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.3611pt}{\resizebox{566377}{407778}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.95276pt}{$\scriptstyle\hbox{\hskip we obtain a morphism

\varphi_{k^{\prime}}\colon F(k^{\prime})\to A

by composing
{F}_{{k^{\prime}},{{\uparrow}\,x}}\colon F(k^{\prime})\to F({\uparrow}\,x)
with

. Note that the definition of
\varphi_{k^{\prime}}
does not depend on the choice of

because

is codirected, and
\{\varphi_{k^{\prime}}\colon F(k^{\prime})\to A\mid k^{\prime}\ll k\}
is a compatible cocone over the restriction of
\kappa^{*}F
to

(recall that, for all

,

).
As

preserves directed colimits, we have

\kappa^{*}F(k)\cong\colim_{k^{\prime}\ll k}\kappa^{*}F(k^{\prime}).

More precisely, the cocone
({F}_{{k^{\prime}},{k}}\colon\kappa^{*}F(k^{\prime})\to\kappa^{*}F(k))_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94443pt}{\resizebox{652442}{582541}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip is a colimit of the restriction of

to

.
Thus, there is a unique mediating morphism
\mu\colon F(k)\to A
satisfying
\varphi_{k^{\prime}}=\mu\circ{F}_{{k^{\prime}},{k}}
for all
k^{\prime}\ll k
. It remains to show that
\mu
satisfies
\psi_{x}=\mu\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}
for all
x\in k
and is unique with this property.
Fix an arbitrary
x\in k
. By
item

(d)
in
Lemma

5.7
there is
k^{\prime}\ll k
such that
x\in k^{\prime}
. It follows that
{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}={F}_{{k^{\prime}},{k}}\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k^{\prime}}}
and so

\mu\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}=\mu\circ{F}_{{k^{\prime}},{k}}\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k^{\prime}}}=\varphi_{k^{\prime}}\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k^{\prime}}},

which coincides with
\psi_{x}
. To deduce that
\mu
is unique with this property, it is enough to note that any
\nu
satisfying
\psi_{x}=\nu\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}
for all
x\in k
must satisfy
\varphi_{k^{\prime}}=\nu\circ{F}_{{k^{\prime}},{k}}
for all
k^{\prime}\ll k
. Just observe that, as mentioned above,
k^{\prime}\ll k
entails the existence of
x\in k
such that
k^{\prime}\subseteq{\uparrow}\,x
. Thus,

\nu\circ{F}_{{k^{\prime}},{k}}=\nu\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}\circ{F}_{{k^{\prime}},{{\uparrow}\,x}}=\psi_{x}\circ{F}_{{k^{\prime}},{{\uparrow}\,x}}=\varphi_{k^{\prime}}.

To show that
2
implies
1
, suppose that
F\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathsf{C}
is such that
\lambda^{*}F
preserves codirected limits and
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism.
Item

(a)
in
Lemma

6.7
applied to
\lambda^{*}F
shows that
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}\lambda^{*}F
preserves directed colimits. Because
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}\lambda^{*}F
is naturally isomorphic to
\kappa^{*}F
via
\kappa^{*}(\varepsilon_{F})
, we see that
\kappa^{*}F\in\omega\text{-}\colim[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
.

It remains to prove that
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism. That is, for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}
,

(\tilde{\eta}_{F})_{\varphi}\colon F(\varphi)\to(\Ran_{\kappa}\kappa^{*}F)(\varphi)

is an isomorphism.
In view of the formula for pointwise right Kan extensions,
(\tilde{\eta}_{F})_{\varphi}
can be identified with the unique mediating morphism

F(\varphi)\to\lim_{\varphi\subseteq k}{F(k)}

for
k
which ranges over
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
. If
\varphi
belongs to the image of
\kappa
(i.e.,
\varphi
is a Scott-open filter) then this mediating morphism is clearly an isomorphism. Hence it suffices to prove that, for all
x\in L
, the following arrow is an isomorphism:

F({\uparrow}\,x)\to\lim_{x\in k}{F(k)}.

Let
\{\psi_{k}\colon A\to F(k)\mid x\in k\}
be a compatible cone in
\mathsf{C}
over the restriction of
F
to
\{k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\mid x\in k\}
. Fix an arbitrary
y\ll x
. By
item

(a)
in
Lemma

5.7
, there is a Scott-open filter
k
such that
x\in k\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y
. Hence we can define an arrow

\varphi_{y}\colon A\to F({\uparrow}\,y)

as the composition of
\psi_{k}\colon A\to F(k)
and the restriction map
{F}_{{k},{{\uparrow}\,y}}\colon F(k)\to F({\uparrow}\,y)
.
Note that the definition of
\varphi_{y}
does not depend on the choice of
k
because

0.65971pt}$}}}}}}}}\,x\}
. As
\lambda^{*}F
preserves codirected limits, we have
\lambda^{*}F(x)\cong\lim_{y\ll x}{\lambda^{*}F(y)}
and so
F({\uparrow}\,x)\cong\lim_{y\ll x}{F({\uparrow}\,y)}
. Thus the compatible cone above induces a unique arrow
\mu\colon A\to F({\uparrow}\,x)
such that
\varphi_{y}={F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{{\uparrow}\,y}}\circ\mu
for all
y\ll x
. It remains to prove that
\mu
satisfies
\psi_{k}={F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}\circ\mu
for all Scott-open filters
k
containing
x
and is unique with this property. Fix an arbitrary
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
such that
x\in k
. By
item

(b)
in
Lemma

5.7
there is
y\ll x
such that
y\in k
. In particular,
{\uparrow}\,x\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y\subseteq k
and therefore

{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}\circ\mu={F}_{{{\uparrow}\,y},{k}}\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{{\uparrow}\,y}}\circ\mu={F}_{{{\uparrow}\,y},{k}}\circ\varphi_{y},

which coincides with
\psi_{k}
. To see that
\mu
is unique with this property, it suffices to note that any
\nu
satisfying
\psi_{k}={F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}\circ\nu
for all
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
such that
x\in k
must satisfy
\varphi_{y}={F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{{\uparrow}\,y}}\circ\nu
for all
y\ll x
. Just recall that, as pointed out above, whenever
y\ll x
there is
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
satisfying
{\uparrow}\,x\subseteq k\subseteq{\uparrow}\,y
. Hence,

{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{{\uparrow}\,y}}\circ\nu={F}_{{k},{{\uparrow}\,y}}\circ{F}_{{{\uparrow}\,x},{k}}\circ\nu={F}_{{k},{{\uparrow}\,y}}\circ\psi_{k}=\varphi_{y}.\qed

Recall that
L
is an arbitrary domain and
\mathsf{C}
is a bicomplete category. The next proposition provides a description of the objects that are fixed by the adjunction in
eq.

7
.

Proposition 6.9
.

The adjoint pair of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{*}\Ran_{\kappa}}
\top
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}}

restricts to an equivalence
\omega\text{-}\colim[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]\simeq\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathsf{F}_{1}
the full subcategory of
[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]
defined by those functors
F
such that
\lambda^{*}F\in\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
and
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism. We claim that the adjunction

\Lan_{\lambda}\dashv\lambda^{*}\colon[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]\to[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]

restricts to an equivalence between
\mathsf{F}_{1}
and
\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
.

By definition of
\mathsf{F}_{1}
,
\lambda^{*}
restricts to a functor
\mathsf{F}_{1}\to\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
. To see that
\Lan_{\lambda}
restricts to a functor
{\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]\to\mathsf{F}_{1}}
, consider an arbitrary
G\in\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
. Then
\eta_{G}\colon G\cong\lambda^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G
by
item

(a)
in
Lemma

6.1
, and so we see that
\lambda^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G
belongs to
\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
. Furthermore, using again the fact that
\eta
is a natural isomorphism, it follows from the triangle identities for adjunctions that
\varepsilon\Lan_{\lambda}
is a natural isomorphism. In particular,
\varepsilon_{\Lan_{\lambda}G}
is an isomorphism. Hence,
\Lan_{\lambda}G\in\mathsf{F}_{1}
.

This shows that the adjunction
\Lan_{\lambda}\dashv\lambda^{*}
restricts to an adjunction between
\mathsf{F}_{1}
and
\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
. In turn, the latter adjunction is an equivalence because all objects of
\mathsf{F}_{1}
are fixed by definition, and all objects of
\omega\text{-}\lim[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]
are fixed by
item

(a)
in
Lemma

6.1
.

Now, let
\mathsf{F}_{2}
be the full subcategory of
[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]
defined by those functors
F
such that
\kappa^{*}F\in\omega\text{-}\colim[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
and
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism. By similar reasoning to the one above, the adjunction
\kappa^{*}\dashv\Ran_{\kappa}\colon[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]\to[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]
restricts to an equivalence between
\omega\text{-}\colim[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
and
\mathsf{F}_{2}
.

Finally, the statement follows by noting that
\mathsf{F}_{1}=\mathsf{F}_{2}
by
Lemma

6.8
.
∎

Suppose for a moment that
\mathsf{C}
is a bicomplete regular category and the domain
L
is a stably continuous lattice. Then
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
is also a stably continuous lattice; in particular, a complete lattice. So, we can consider the category
\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})
of
\mathsf{C}
-valued
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves over
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}}
. This is a full subcategory of
[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]
and we have a composite functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\mathsf{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{*}\circ\Ran_{\kappa}}

In order to characterise the image of this functor, we introduce the notion of
\Omega
-sheaf
.
Intuitively, whereas
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves on a (stably compact) space
X
are defined on the lattice
\mathcal{K}(X)
of compact saturated subsets of
X
,
\Omega
-sheaves are defined on the frame
\Omega(X)
of opens of
X
—hence the nomenclature. The relation between
\Omega
-sheaves and ordinary sheaves is explained in
Proposition

6.14
and
Remark

6.15
.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a category and let
P
be a complete lattice.
A
\mathsf{D}
-valued
\Omega
-sheaf
on
P
is a functor
F\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{D}
that satisfies the following properties:

(
\Omega
1)

F(\bot)
is a subterminal object of
\mathsf{D}
, i.e. the unique arrow
F(\bot)\to\mathbf{1}
is monic.

(
\Omega
2)

For all
x,y\in P
, the following is a pullback square in
\mathsf{D}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x\vee y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(x\wedge y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{x\vee y},{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{x\vee y},{y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{x},{x\wedge y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}_{{y},{x\wedge y}}}

(
\Omega
3)

F
preserves codirected limits. I.e., for all directed subsets
D\subseteq P
, the cone
({F}_{{\bigvee D},{p}}\colon F(\bigvee D)\to F(p))_{p\in D}
is a limit of the restriction of
F
to
D
.

We denote by
\OSh(P,\mathsf{D})
the full subcategory of
[P^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{D}]
defined by the
\Omega
-sheaves.

Remark 6.11
.

Note that conditions
(

Ω

1)
and
(

Ω

2)
coincide, respectively, with conditions
(K1)
and
(K2)
in the definition of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf. By contrast,
(

Ω

3)
is
dual
to
(K3)
.

.

Lemma

6.6

P

.

Lemma 6.13
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{D}
is a category and
P
is a complete lattice. The following statements are equivalent for all functors
F\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{D}
:

(1)

F
satisfies
(

Ω

2)
and
(

Ω

3)
.

(2)

For every non-empty subset
S\subseteq P
that is closed under binary meets, the cone
({F}_{{\bigvee S},{y}}\colon F(\bigvee S)\to F(y))_{y\in S}
is a limit of the restriction of
F
to
S
.

Proof.

Suppose
1
holds and let
S\subseteq P
be a non-empty subset closed under binary meets. The set
T\subseteq P
obtained by closing
S
under binary joins is directed and so, by
(

Ω

3)
, the cone
({F}_{{\bigvee T},{y}}\colon F(\bigvee T)\to F(y))_{y\in T}
is a limit of the restriction of
F
to
T
. It follows from
(

Ω

2)
that the cone
({F}_{{\bigvee S},{y}}\colon F(\bigvee S)\to F(y))_{y\in S}
is a limit of the restriction of
F
to
S
. Just observe that
\bigvee S=\bigvee T
and condition
(

Ω

2)
allows to extend any compatible cone over the restriction of
F
to
S
to a unique compatible cone over the restriction of
F
to
T
.

Conversely, assume that
2
holds. Then
(

Ω

2)
follows by setting
S\coloneqq\{x,y,x\land y\}
.
With regards to
(

Ω

3)
, let
D\subseteq P
be a directed subset and let
E
be the closure of
D
under binary meets.
As
D
is non-empty, so is
E
. Also, since
D
is a cofinal subset of
E
, we have
\bigvee{D}=\bigvee{E}
.
By
item

2
, the cone
({F}_{{\bigvee D},{y}}\colon F(\bigvee D)\to F(y))_{y\in E}
is a limit of the restriction of
F
to
E
.
Moreover, a straightforward argument that uses the fact that
D
is a directed cofinal subset of
E
shows that
({F}_{{\bigvee D},{y}}\colon F(\bigvee D)\to F(y))_{y\in D}
is a limit of the restriction of
F
to
D
.
∎

Recall that a sheaf of sets on a topological space
X
can be characterised as a presheaf
F\colon\Omega(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
such that, for every set of opens
S\subseteq\Omega(X)
that is closed under binary intersections, the cone

({F}_{{\bigcup S},{U}}\colon F\big(\bigcup S\big)\to F(U))_{U\in S}

is a limit of the restriction of
F
to
S
. For
S=\emptyset
, this amounts to saying that
F(\emptyset)
is a terminal object of
\mathsf{Set}
, i.e. a one-element set, which is a strengthening of
(

Ω

1)
. More generally, for any frame
M
, a sheaf of sets over
M
is a presheaf
F\colon M^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
such that, for every set
S\subseteq M
closed under binary meets, the cone

({F}_{{\bigvee S},{a}}\colon F\big(\bigvee S\big)\to F(a))_{a\in S}

is a limit of the restriction of
F
to
S
.

The previous description of sheaves as “limit-preserving presheaves” remains valid when the category of sets is replaced with any variety of (finitary) algebras, and has been exploited to propose a notion of sheaf with values in an arbitrary category
\mathsf{D}
—simply by replacing
\mathsf{Set}
with
\mathsf{D}
—see e.g.
[
25
]
. Adopting this notion of
\mathsf{D}
-valued sheaf on a frame, we have the following immediate consequence of
Lemma

6.13
:

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a category and let
M
be a frame.
The following statements are equivalent for all presheaves
F\colon M^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{D}
:

(1)

F
is a
\mathsf{D}
-valued sheaf.

(2)

F
is a
\mathsf{D}
-valued
\Omega
-sheaf such that
F(\emptyset)
is a terminal object of
\mathsf{D}
.

Thus the notion of
\Omega
-sheaf of sets over a frame (and, in particular, over a topological space) coincides with the classical notion of sheaf of sets, with the only exception that we allow the empty presheaf. This is the content of the following remark:

Remark 6.15
.

Let
M
be a frame.
When
\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{Set}
, the only proper subterminal object is the empty set. Thus, any
\Omega
-sheaf
F\colon M^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
that is not a sheaf satisfies
F(\bot)=\emptyset
. But for every
a\in M
there is a restriction function
{F}_{{a},{\bot}}\colon F(a)\to F(\bot)=\emptyset
, which implies that
F(a)=\emptyset
. Thus, a presheaf of sets
M^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
is an
\Omega
-sheaf if, and only if, it is either a sheaf or the initial presheaf (i.e., the constant presheaf of value
\emptyset
).

A similar reasoning applies when
\mathsf{D}
is any variety of algebras whose signature contains no constant symbols, e.g. the variety of semigroups. On the other hand, if
\mathsf{D}
is a variety of algebras whose signature contains at least one constant symbol, then it admits no proper subterminal objects (just observe that any algebra in
\mathsf{D}
has at least one element). In this case, the notions of sheaf and
\Omega
-sheaf coincide for any presheaf
M^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{D}
.

We will now proceed to compare the notions of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf and
\Omega
-sheaf. To this end, we shall suppose from now on that
\mathsf{C}
is a bicomplete regular category and
L
is a stably continuous lattice.

Definition 6.16
.

We consider the following full subcategories
\mathsf{A}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mathsf{A}_{\Omega}
of
[\mathcal{F},\mathsf{C}]
:

•

\mathsf{A}_{\mathcal{K}}
consists of those
F
such that
\kappa^{*}F
is a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf and
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism.

•

\mathsf{A}_{\Omega}
consists of those
F
such that
\lambda^{*}F
is an
\Omega
-sheaf and
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism.

The next lemma follows by reasoning as in the proof of
Proposition

6.9
:

Lemma 6.17
.

The following statements hold:

(a)

The adjunction
\kappa^{*}\dashv\Ran_{\kappa}
restricts to an equivalence
{\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\simeq\mathsf{A}_{\mathcal{K}}}
.

(b)

The adjunction
\Lan_{\lambda}\dashv\lambda^{*}
restricts to an equivalence
\OSh(L,\mathsf{C})\simeq\mathsf{A}_{\Omega}
.

Lemma 6.18
.

\mathsf{A}_{\mathcal{K}}
is a (full) subcategory of
\mathsf{A}_{\Omega}
.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary functor
F\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathsf{C}
that belongs to
\mathsf{A}_{\mathcal{K}}
. We must prove that
\lambda^{*}F
is an
\Omega
-sheaf and
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism. Since
\tilde{\eta}_{F}\colon F\to\Ran_{\kappa}\kappa^{*}F
is an isomorphism, the formula for pointwise right Kan extensions implies that, for all
x\in L
,

\lambda^{*}F(x)\cong\lim_{x\in k}\kappa^{*}F(k)

where
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
. Just observe that
x\in k
if and only if
{\uparrow}\,x\subseteq k
in
\mathcal{F}
.

Condition
(

Ω

1)
in
Definition

6.10
is trivially satisfied, since the unique (Scott-open) filter containing the bottom element of
L
is the improper one, which is the bottom element of
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}}
. Hence
\lambda^{*}F(\bot)\cong\kappa^{*}F(L)
, which is subterminal because
\kappa^{*}F
satisfies
(K1)
.
For
(

Ω

2)
, for all
x,y\in L
we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}F(x)\times_{\lambda^{*}F(x\wedge y)}\lambda^{*}F(y)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{x\in k}{\kappa^{*}F(k)}\times_{\lim{\kappa^{*}F(k\vee k^{\prime})}}\lim_{y\in k^{\prime}}{\kappa^{*}F(k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{x\in k,\,y\in k^{\prime}}{(\kappa^{*}F(k)\times_{\kappa^{*}F(k\vee k^{\prime})}\kappa^{*}F(k^{\prime}))}

\kappa^{*}F
satisfies
(K2)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{x\in k,\,y\in k^{\prime}}{\kappa^{*}F(k\wedge k^{\prime})}

Lemma

5.10
and coinitiality
4
4
footnotemark:

4

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{x\vee y\in l}{\kappa^{*}F(l)}

\displaystyle\cong\lambda^{*}F(x\vee y)

where in the first step we used the fact that
\{l\mid x\wedge y\in l\}=\{k\vee k^{\prime}\mid x\in k,\,y\in k^{\prime}\}
and so

\lim_{x\wedge y\in l}{\kappa^{*}F(l)}\cong\lim_{x\in k,\,y\in k^{\prime}}{\kappa^{*}F(k\vee k^{\prime})}.

Finally, since
\kappa^{*}F
preserves directed colimits (by definition of
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf) and
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism,
Lemma

6.8
entails that
\lambda^{*}F
satisfies
(

Ω

3)
and
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism.

∎

We record the following immediate consequence of
Lemmas

6.17
and
6.18
. Recall that
L
denotes an arbitrary stably continuous lattice, and
\mathsf{C}
a bicomplete regular category.

Proposition 6.19
.

There is a fully faithful functor

(9)

\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\to\OSh(L,\mathsf{C})

given by the following composition:

\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\simeq\mathsf{A}_{\mathcal{K}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{A}_{\Omega}\simeq\OSh(L,\mathsf{C}).

Next, we shall see that the fully faithful functor in the previous proposition is an equivalence of categories whenever directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
commute with finite limits.

Remark 6.20
.

Directed colimits commute with finite limits in
\mathsf{Set}
(more generally, in any Grothendieck topos), as well as in algebraic categories (i.e., categories of models of Lawvere theories, or equivalently varieties of finitary algebras); see e.g.
[
7
, Corollary 3.4.3]
.

Furthermore, suppose
\mathsf{D}
is a Barr-exact category with a regular generator that admits all small copowers. Then, by a result of Vitale
[
45
]
, directed colimits in
\mathsf{D}
exist and commute with finite limits if, and only if,
\mathsf{D}
is equivalent to the
localization
(i.e., a reflective subcategory such that the reflector preserves finite limits) of an algebraic category.

The following theorem provides an equivalence between
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves and
\Omega
-sheaves, and is akin to a result of Lurie for sheaves on locally compact Hausdorff spaces with values in
\infty
-categories
[
35
, Corollary 7.3.4.10]
. The two results are incomparable: we work with ordinary categories but consider, more generally, sheaves on stably continuous lattices.

Theorem 6.21
.

If directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
commute with finite limits, there is an equivalence of categories
\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\simeq\OSh(L,\mathsf{C})
.

Proof.

Consider the fully faithful functor
\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\to\OSh(L,\mathsf{C})
in
eq.

9
. In view of the definition of the latter, it suffices to show that
\mathsf{A}_{\Omega}
is a (full) subcategory of
\mathsf{A}_{\mathcal{K}}
, for then
\mathsf{A}_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathsf{A}_{\Omega}
. To this end, fix an arbitrary functor
F\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathsf{C}
in
\mathsf{A}_{\Omega}
. We must show that
\kappa^{*}F
is a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf and
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism.
Since the component of the counit
\varepsilon_{F}\colon\Lan_{\lambda}\lambda^{*}F\to F
is an isomorphism, for all
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
we have

\kappa^{*}F(k)\cong\colim_{x\in k}\lambda^{*}F(x)

by the formula for pointwise left Kan extensions. Note that the colimit above is directed because
k
is codirected and
\lambda^{*}F
is contravariant.

Clearly,
\kappa^{*}F
satisfies condition
(K1)
in
Definition

3.3
.
Just observe that the bottom element of
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}}
is the improper filter
L
, and so every arrow in the colimit cocone

\{\lambda^{*}F(x)\to\kappa^{*}F(L)\mid x\in L\}

factors through
\lambda^{*}F(\bot)\to\kappa^{*}F(L)
. Hence
\kappa^{*}F(L)\cong\lambda^{*}F(\bot)
, which is subterminal because
\lambda^{*}F
satisfies
(

Ω

1)
.
For
(K2)
, using the fact that directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
commute with finite limits, for all
k,l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
we have

\displaystyle\kappa^{*}F(k)\times_{\kappa^{*}F(k\vee l)}\kappa^{*}F(l)

\displaystyle\cong\colim_{x\in k}{\lambda^{*}F(x)}\times_{\colim{\lambda^{*}F(x\wedge y)}}\colim_{y\in l}{F(y)}

\displaystyle\cong\colim_{x\in k,\,y\in l}{(\lambda^{*}F(x)\times_{\lambda^{*}F(x\wedge y)}\lambda^{*}F(y))}

\lambda^{*}F
satisfies
(

Ω

2)

\displaystyle\cong\colim_{x\in k,\,y\in l}{\lambda^{*}F(x\vee y)}

\displaystyle\cong\colim_{z\in k\wedge l}{\lambda^{*}F(z)}

\displaystyle\cong\kappa^{*}F(k\wedge l)

where in the first step we used the fact that the supremum of the Scott-open filters
k
and
l
is
{\uparrow}\,\{x\wedge y\mid x\in k,\,y\in l\}
and thus, by coinitiality,

\colim_{z\in k\vee l}{\lambda^{*}F(z)}\cong\colim_{x\in k,\,y\in l}{\lambda^{*}F(x\wedge y)}.

Similarly, the penultimate step holds because
k\wedge l=\{x\vee y\mid x\in k,\,y\in l\}
.
Moreover, because
\lambda^{*}F
preserves codirected limits (by definition of
\Omega
-sheaf) and
\varepsilon_{F}
is an isomorphism,
Lemma

6.8
shows that
\kappa^{*}F
satisfies
(K3)
and
\tilde{\eta}_{F}
is an isomorphism.
∎

The equivalence of categories in the previous theorem induces an equivalence of sheaf representations in the following sense.

Definition 6.22
.

For every object
A
of a category
\mathsf{D}
, an
\Omega
-sheaf representation
of
A
over a complete lattice
P
is a pair
(F,\varphi)
where
F\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{D}
is an
\Omega
-sheaf and
\varphi\colon A\to F(\top)
an isomorphism in
\mathsf{D}
.

We denote by
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(P,\mathsf{D})
the category of
\Omega
-sheaf representations of
A
over
P
; a morphism
(F,\varphi)\to(G,\psi)
in this category is a natural transformation
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G
such that
\alpha_{\top}\circ\varphi=\psi
.

Theorem 6.23
.

If directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
commute with finite limits, then for any
A\in\mathsf{C}
there is an equivalence of categories
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\simeq\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})
.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
and an
\Omega
-sheaf representation
(F,\varphi)
of
A
over
L
. The top element of
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}}
is the filter
\{\top\}
, where
\top
is the top element of
L
(note that
\{\top\}
is Scott-open because
\top\ll\top
in a stably continuous lattice). We have

\lambda^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F(\top)=\Lan_{\lambda}F(\{\top\})=\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F(\{\top\})

and so, by
item

(a)
in
Lemma

6.1
, we obtain an isomorphism
(\eta_{F})_{\top}
from
F(\top)
to
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F(\{\top\})
.
The composite arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}\colon A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F(\{\top\})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta_{F})_{\top}}

is then an isomorphism and the pair
(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F,\varphi^{*})
is a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representation of
A
over
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}}
. Further, if
\alpha\colon(F,\varphi)\to(G,\psi)
is a morphism in
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(P,\mathsf{C})
then

\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}(\alpha)\colon(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F,\varphi^{*})\to(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G,\psi^{*})

is a morphism in
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})
. Just observe that the square in the following diagram commutes by naturality of
\eta
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F(\{\top\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\top)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G(\{\top\})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta_{F})_{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}(\alpha))_{\{\top\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\eta_{G})_{\top}}

and so
(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}(\alpha))_{\{\top\}}\circ\varphi^{*}=\psi^{*}
.

This yields a functor

(10)

\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})\to\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C}).

We claim that the latter is an equivalence of categories.
It follows from (the proof of)
Theorem

6.21
that
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}\colon\OSh(L,\mathsf{C})\to\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})
is an equivalence.
Hence the functor in
eq.

10
is faithful. To see that it is full, fix an arbitrary morphism

\beta\colon(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F,\varphi^{*})\to(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}G,\psi^{*})

in
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})
. Since
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}
is full, there is
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G
such that
{\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}(\alpha)=\beta}
.
Note that

Naturality of
\eta

\displaystyle(\eta_{G})_{\top}\circ\alpha_{\top}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}(\alpha))_{\{\top\}}\circ(\eta_{F})_{\top}\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\beta_{\{\top\}}\circ\varphi^{*}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}

\displaystyle=(\eta_{G})_{\top}\circ\psi

and so
\alpha_{\top}\circ\varphi=\psi
because
(\eta_{G})_{\top}
is an isomorphism. It follows that
\alpha
is a morphism of
\Omega
-sheaf representations and thus the functor in
eq.

10
is full. Finally, fix an arbitrary object
(G,\psi)\in\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})
. Because
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}
is essentially surjective, there exist an
\Omega
-sheaf
F\in\OSh(L,\mathsf{C})
and a natural isomorphism
\delta\colon\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F\Rightarrow G
. Let

\varphi\coloneqq(\delta_{\{\top\}}\circ(\eta_{F})_{\top})^{-1}\circ\psi\colon A\to F(\top).

Then
(F,\varphi)
is an object of
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})
whose image under the functor in
eq.

10
is isomorphic to
(G,\psi)
. We conclude that the latter functor is an equivalence of categories.
∎

The classical notion of soft sheaf can be extended to arbitrary
\Omega
-sheaves:

Definition 6.24
.

Let
P
be a complete lattice and let
\mathsf{D}
be a category admitting directed colimits. An
\Omega
-sheaf
F\colon P^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{D}
is
soft
if, for all Scott-open filters
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(P)
, the canonical colimit arrow

F(\top)\to\colim_{x\in k}{F(x)}

is a regular epimorphism.

An
\Omega
-sheaf representation
(F,\varphi)
is said to be
soft
if
F
is a soft
\Omega
-sheaf. The full subcategory of
\mathrm{Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(P,\mathsf{D})
defined by the soft
\Omega
-sheaf representations is denoted by

\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(P,\mathsf{D}).

Remark 6.25
.

In the case where
P=\Omega(X)
for a sober space
X
, and
\mathsf{D}
is
\mathsf{Set}
—or, more generally, any variety of finitary algebras—the Hofmann–Mislove theorem implies that the notion of softness introduced in
Definition

6.24
coincides with the ordinary one (always with the
caveat
outlined in
Remark

6.15
). Cf. e.g.
[
21
, Remark 3.3]
.

Remark 6.26
.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be
\mathsf{Set}
or, more generally, any variety of algebras. Fix an object
A\in\mathsf{D}
and assume that
F\colon\Omega(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{D}
is a soft
\Omega
-sheaf representation of
A
over a sober space
X
. Reasoning as in
Remark

6.4
, we see that for all
x\in X
the canonical arrow

A\to\colim_{x\in U}{F(U)}

whose codomain is the stalk of
F
at
x
is a regular epimorphism. This exhibits each stalk of
F
as a quotient of
A
.

Lemma 6.27
.

Suppose that directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
commute with finite limits.
The following statements are equivalent for all functors
F\colon L^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
:

(1)

F
is a soft
\Omega
-sheaf.

(2)

\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F
is a soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary functor
F\colon L^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
. In view of
Theorem

6.23
,
F
is an
\Omega
-sheaf over
L
precisely when
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F
is a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf over
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
F
is soft (as an
\Omega
-sheaf) if, and only if,
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F
is soft (as a
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf).

Recall that the top element of
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}}
is
\{\top\}
, where
\top
is the top element of
L
. Hence,
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F
is soft precisely when, for all
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
, the arrow

{\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F}_{{\{\top\}},{k}}\colon(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F)(\{\top\})\to(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F)(k)

is a regular epimorphism. Moreover, recall from
Remark

6.3
that, for all
l\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
,
(\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F)(l)\cong\colim_{x\in l}{F(x)}
. Under this isomorphism,
{\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F}_{{\{\top\}},{k}}
can be identified with the canonical colimit arrow

F(\top)\to\colim_{x\in k}{F(x)}.

Therefore,
\kappa^{*}\Lan_{\lambda}F
is soft if, and only if, so is
F
.
∎

Remark 6.28
.

Even if direct colimits in
\mathsf{C}
fail to commute with finite limits, by
Proposition

6.19
there is a full and faithful functor
\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\hookrightarrow\OSh(L,\mathsf{C})
. The second part of the proof of
Lemma

6.27
, combined with
Proposition

6.9
, then shows that this functor sends soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves to soft
\Omega
-sheaves.

Combining the previous observations, we obtain an equivalence between soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations and soft
\Omega
-sheaf representations:

Proposition 6.29
.

If directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
commute with finite limits, then for any
A\in\mathsf{C}
there is an equivalence of categories
\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\simeq\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})
.

Proof.

By (the proof of)
Theorem

6.23
, combined with
Lemma

6.27
.
∎

Remark 6.30
.

Under the assumptions of
Proposition

6.29
, the category
\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})
of soft
\Omega
-sheaf representations of
A
over
L
is a (large) preorder. This follows from
Proposition

6.29
, recalling that
\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})
is a preorder by
item

(b)
in
Lemma

4.5
.

We can finally state our main result, which characterises soft
\Omega
-sheaf representations for a broad class of regular categories. Recall that
L
denotes an arbitrary stably continuous lattice, and
\mathsf{C}
a bicomplete regular category.

Theorem 6.31
.

Suppose that directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
commute with finite limits, and let
A\in\mathsf{C}
.
Let
\mathsf{M}
be the (large) sub-preorder of
[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\RegEpi{A}]
consisting of those maps that preserve finite infima and arbitrary suprema, and whose images consist of pairwise ker-commuting elements. There is an equivalence of categories

\mathsf{M}\simeq\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C}).

Proof.

By
Theorem

4.7
and
Proposition

6.29
.
∎

In the same vein of
Corollary

4.8
, we state a consequence of
Theorem

6.31
obtained by taking the poset reflections of the categories involved, assuming that
\mathsf{C}
is Barr-exact. Recall that
\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})\rrbracket
denotes the poset reflection of
\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})
; the objects of
\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})\rrbracket
are isomorphism classes of soft
\Omega
-sheaf representations of
A
over
L
.

Corollary 6.32
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{C}
is Barr-exact and directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
commute with finite limits, and let
A\in\mathsf{C}
.
Let
\mathsf{N}
be the (large) sub-poset of
[\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\Equiv{A}]
consisting of those maps that preserve finite infima and arbitrary suprema, and whose images consist of pairwise commuting equivalence relations. There is an order isomorphism

\mathsf{N}\cong\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})\rrbracket.

Proof.

Note that two (large) posets that are equivalent as categories must be order isomorphic. Thus, by
Theorem

6.31
, there is an order isomorphism
\llbracket\mathsf{M}\rrbracket\cong\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C})\rrbracket
. In turn, as pointed out in the proof of
Corollary

4.8
,
\llbracket\mathsf{M}\rrbracket\cong\mathsf{N}
.
∎

Theorems

6.31
and
6.32
are generalisations of
[
21
, Theorem 3.10]
from the framework of varieties of finitary algebras to that of regular and Barr-exact categories, respectively. We end this section with some remarks concerning the previous results.

Remark 6.33
.

As with
Corollary

4.8
, if
\mathsf{C}
is a well-powered category then all posets in the statement of
Corollary

6.32
are small (cf.
Remark

4.10
).

Remark 6.34
.

Even when directed colimits in
\mathsf{C}
do not commute with finite limits, and so
Theorem

6.31
does not apply, by
Proposition

6.19
there is a full and faithful functor

\KSh(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\hookrightarrow\OSh(L,\mathsf{C}).

The latter induces, for every object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
, a full and faithful functor

\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\mathcal{K}}(\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{C})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C}).

Cf.
Remark

6.28
.
Hence, in view of
Theorem

4.7
, there is an order embedding

\mathsf{M}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(L,\mathsf{C}).

In particular, every map
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\to\RegEpi{A}
that preserves finite infima and arbitrary suprema, and whose image consists of pairwise ker-commuting elements, induces a soft
\Omega
-sheaf representation of
A
over
L
. In a similar fashion, if
\mathsf{C}
is Barr-exact and there exists a map
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\to\Equiv{A}
that preserves finite infima and arbitrary suprema, and whose image consists of pairwise commuting equivalence relations, then
A
admits a soft
\Omega
-sheaf representation over
L
.

Remark 6.35
.

Throughout this paper we have adopted an “external” perspective, whereby an object of a regular category
\mathsf{C}
is studied via (pre)sheaves
L^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{C}
. However it is well known that, in many situations, the appropriate notion of sheaf of
\mathsf{C}
-objects is given by an “internal
\mathsf{C}
-object” in the category of sheaves of sets. Whereas the internal and external perspectives coincide when
\mathsf{C}
is the category of models of a finitary algebraic theory (i.e.,
\mathsf{C}
is a variety of finitary algebras), they may differ for arbitrary first-order theories---consider e.g. the elementary theory of fields.
7
7

7

This is due to the fact that the global section functor does not preserve the validity of all first-order sentences, but only of
Cartesian theories
, cf. e.g.
[
28
, §V.1.12]
.
We do not know if, in general, our results can be adapted to the internal perspective—whenever the latter is available.

7.
Examples

7.1.
The dual of compact ordered spaces

A
compact ordered space
is a compact space
X
equipped with a partial order that is closed in the product topology of
X\times X
. Compact ordered spaces were first introduced by Nachbin in his monograph
[
39
]
.

Let
\mathsf{CompOrd}
denote the category of compact ordered spaces and continuous monotone maps between them. For any object
X
of
\mathsf{CompOrd}
we write
X_{*}
for the same object, but this time regarded as an object of the opposite category
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
. In this subsection, we shall investigate soft sheaf representations of objects of
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Recall that regular monomorphisms in
\mathsf{CompOrd}
can be identified, up to isomorphism, with the closed subsets with the induced order
[
27
, Theorem 2.6]
.
Moreover, it follows from the main result of
[
1
]
that
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a Barr-exact category (see also
[
2
]
for a direct proof).
Thus, for any compact ordered space
X
, there is an order isomorphism between
(\Equiv{X_{*}})^{\mathrm{op}}
and the coframe of closed subsets of
X
. Equivalently,

\Equiv{X_{*}}\cong\Omega(X).

For the next lemma recall that, by
Corollary

2.5
and
Remark

2.10
, for any object
A
of a Barr-exact category admitting pushouts, the poset
\Equiv{A}
is a (bounded) lattice. Further, given
\vartheta\in\Equiv{A}
, we write

A\twoheadrightarrow A/{\vartheta}

for its coequaliser—provided it exists. If
\vartheta_{1}\leq\vartheta_{2}
in
\Equiv{A}
, the universal property of the coequaliser entails the existence of a unique regular epimorphism
A/{\vartheta_{1}}\twoheadrightarrow A/{\vartheta_{2}}
through which
A\twoheadrightarrow A/{\vartheta_{2}}
factors.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
A
be an object of a finitely cocomplete Barr-exact category, and let
\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}\in\Equiv{A}
. Then
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
commute if, and only if, the following diagram (whose arrows are the canonical ones) is a pullback.

(11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/{(\vartheta_{1}\wedge\vartheta_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/{\vartheta_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/{\vartheta_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/{(\vartheta_{1}\vee\vartheta_{2})}}

Proof.

The square on the left-hand side below is a pushout by
Lemma

2.3
, where
f
and
g
are the coequalisers of
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
, respectively, and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
are the canonical arrows induced by the universal property of
f
and
g
, respectively.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/{\vartheta_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/{\vartheta_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/{(\vartheta_{1}\vee\vartheta_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\vartheta_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\vartheta_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/(\vartheta_{1}\vee\vartheta_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{2}}

Let
P
be the pullback of
\eta_{1}
along
\eta_{2}
, and let
q\colon A\to P
denote the unique arrow making the right-hand diagram above commute.
By
Lemma

3.8
,
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
commute precisely when
q
is a regular epimorphism.

Now, if
q
is a regular epimorphism then it is an infimum of
f
and
g
in
\RegEpi{A}
; so, up to an isomorphism, it coincides with the coequaliser
A\twoheadrightarrow A/(\vartheta_{1}\wedge\vartheta_{2})
. Thus, (
11
) is a pullback square.
Conversely, suppose (
11
) is a pullback.
Then
q
is a coequaliser of
\vartheta_{1}\wedge\vartheta_{2}
, hence a regular epimorphism.
∎

In
[
21
, Lemma 5.4]
, Gehrke and van Gool characterised the pairs of commuting congruences on a bounded distributive lattice
A
in terms of the corresponding closed subsets of the dual
Priestley space
of
A

[
42
]
. The next result extends their characterisation from Priestley spaces to compact ordered spaces.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
X
be a compact ordered space. Let
\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}\in\Equiv{X_{*}}
and let
C_{1},C_{2}
be the corresponding closed subsets of
X
. The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The equivalence relations
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
commute.

(2)

For any
x_{1}\in C_{1}
,
x_{2}\in C_{2}
, if
\{i,j\}=\{1,2\}
and
x_{i}\leq x_{j}
, there exists
z\in C_{1}\cap C_{2}
such that
x_{i}\leq z\leq x_{j}
.

Proof.

The category
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
is cocomplete and Barr-exact (cf.
[
2
]
), thus
Lemma

7.1
entails that
\vartheta_{1}
and
\vartheta_{2}
commute if and only if the following is a pushout in
\mathsf{CompOrd}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}\cap C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}\cup C_{2}}

In turn, this is equivalent to the condition in
item

2
. Cf. e.g.
[
2
, Remark 6]
.
∎

If
X
is a compact ordered space, we can consider the collection of all open subsets of
X
that are downwards closed in the partial order of
X
. The latter forms a topology on the underlying set of
X
, and we shall denote by
X^{\downarrow}
the ensuing topological space. In fact,
X^{\downarrow}
is a stably compact space (cf.
Example

5.12
) and every stably compact space arises in this manner, cf. e.g.
[
33
, Proposition 2.10]
. Similarly for the space
X^{\uparrow}
obtained by considering the topology consisting of the open subsets of
X
that are upwards closed. The space
X^{\uparrow}
is called the
co-compact dual
of
X^{\downarrow}
and there is a frame isomorphism
\Omega(X^{\downarrow})\cong\mathcal{K}(X^{\uparrow})^{\mathrm{op}}
sending an open subset of
X^{\downarrow}
to its complement, cf. e.g.
[
29
, §2.2]
.

The following is a direct generalisation of
[
21
, Definition 5.5]
, from the setting of Priestley spaces to that of compact ordered spaces.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
X,Y
be compact ordered spaces. An
interpolating decomposition of
X
over
Y
is a continuous function
q\colon X\to Y^{\downarrow}
such that, for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, if
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
then there is
z\in X
such that
x_{1}\leq z\leq x_{2}
,
q(x_{1})\leq q(z)
and
q(x_{2})\leq q(z)
.
8
8

8

The inequalities
q(x_{1})\leq q(z)
and
q(x_{2})\leq q(z)
refer to the partial order of
Y
.

If
X,Y
are compact ordered spaces and
{q\colon X\to Y^{\downarrow}}
is a continuous map, denote by

\psi_{q}\colon\Omega(Y^{\downarrow})\to\Equiv{X_{*}}

the composition of the frame homomorphism
\Omega(q)\colon\Omega(Y^{\downarrow})\to\Omega(X)
with the order isomorphism
\Omega(X)\cong\Equiv{X_{*}}
. It is useful to consider the map
\varphi_{q}
order-dual to
\psi_{q}
. Since
\Omega(Y^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{op}}\cong\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})
, we can assume that this order-dual map has type

\varphi_{q}\colon\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})\to(\Equiv{X_{*}})^{\mathrm{op}}.

Note that any compact ordered space is Hausdorff, hence the poset
\mathcal{K}(X)
of compact saturated subsets of
X
coincides with the coframe of its closed subsets. Thus,
\varphi_{q}
can be equivalently described as the composite of the inverse image map
q^{-1}\colon\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})\to\mathcal{K}(X)
with the order isomorphism
\mathcal{K}(X)\cong(\Equiv{X_{*}})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
X,Y
be compact ordered spaces and let
{q\colon X\to Y^{\downarrow}}
be a continuous function. The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The function
q
is an interpolating decomposition of
X
over
Y
.

(2)

Any two equivalence relations in the image of
\psi_{q}\colon\Omega(Y^{\downarrow})\to\Equiv{X_{*}}
commute.

Proof.

We prove, equivalently, that
1
holds if and only if any two elements in the image of
\varphi_{q}
(the map order-dual to
\psi_{q}
) commute.

Assume
1
holds and let
K_{1},K_{2}\in\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})
.
To show that
\varphi_{q}(K_{1})
and
\varphi_{q}(K_{2})
commute, it suffices to prove that the closed sets
C_{i}\coloneqq q^{-1}(K_{i})
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, satisfy the property in
item

2
of
Proposition

7.2
.
Fix arbitrary elements
x_{1}\in C_{1}
and
x_{2}\in C_{2}
such that
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
(if
x_{2}\leq x_{1}
the proof is the same, mutatis mutandis). Because
q
is an interpolating decomposition, there is
z\in X
such that
x_{1}\leq z\leq x_{2}
,
q(x_{1})\leq q(z)
and
q(x_{2})\leq q(z)
. It remains to show that
z\in C_{1}\cap C_{2}
.
As
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are compact saturated subsets of
Y^{\uparrow}
, they are upwards closed in the order of
Y
(cf. e.g.
[
29
, §2.2]
).
Since
q(x_{i})\in K_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, we get
q(z)\in K_{1}\cap K_{2}
and so
z\in C_{1}\cap C_{2}
.

Conversely, suppose any two elements in the image of
\varphi_{q}
commute and let
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
satisfy
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
. Write
y_{i}\coloneqq q(x_{i})
and
C_{i}\coloneqq q^{-1}({\uparrow}\,y_{i}
) for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Clearly,
x_{i}\in C_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Moreover, by definition of
\varphi_{q}
,
C_{i}
is the closed subset of
X
corresponding to the equivalence relation
\varphi_{q}({\uparrow}\,y_{i})
on
X_{*}
. Since
\varphi_{q}({\uparrow}\,y_{1})
and
\varphi_{q}({\uparrow}\,y_{2})
commute, by
Proposition

7.2
there is
z\in C_{1}\cap C_{2}
such that
x_{1}\leq z\leq x_{2}
.
Note that, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,
z\in C_{i}
implies that
q(x_{i})\leq q(z)
.
∎

Let
X,Y
be compact ordered spaces and let
(F,\varphi)
be a soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representation of
X_{*}
over the coframe
\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})
.
Under the order isomorphism in
Corollary

4.8
, the isomorphism class of
(F,\varphi)
corresponds to a frame homomorphism
{\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Equiv{X_{*}}}
. Identifying
\Equiv{X_{*}}
with
\Omega(X)
, and
\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})^{\mathrm{op}}
with
\Omega(Y^{\downarrow})
, we shall denote this frame homomorphism by
\psi_{(F,\varphi)}\colon\Omega(Y^{\downarrow})\to\Omega(X)
. Note that
Y^{\downarrow}
and
X
are sober spaces, so there is a unique continuous function

q_{(F,\varphi)}\colon X\to Y^{\downarrow}

such that
\Omega(q_{(F,\varphi)})=\psi_{(F,\varphi)}
.
The following theorem extends Gehrke and van Gool’s result
[
21
, Theorem 5.7]
from distributive lattices to the dual of compact ordered spaces.

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
X,Y
be compact ordered spaces. The assignment

(F,\varphi)\mapsto q_{(F,\varphi)}

yields a bijection between isomorphism classes of soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations of
X_{*}
over
\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})
and interpolating decompositions of
X
over
Y
.

Proof.

This follows from
Corollary

4.8
and
Proposition

7.4
.
∎

Observe that, for every compact ordered space
X
, there is a fully faithful functor

\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{X_{*}}_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow}),\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{X_{*}}_{\Omega}(\Omega(Y^{\uparrow}),\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}})

from soft
\mathcal{K}
-sheaf representations of
X_{*}
over
\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})
to soft sheaf representations of
X_{*}
over
Y^{\uparrow}
, and this is an equivalence provided that directed colimits commute with finite limits in
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
(cf.
Proposition

6.19
and
Theorem

6.21
). We do not know if the category
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
satisfies the latter property and conjecture that it does not.

Nevertheless, replacing
\mathcal{K}
-sheaves over
\mathcal{K}(Y^{\uparrow})
with ordinary sheaves over
Y^{\uparrow}
in
Theorem

7.5
, we obtain an injective assignment from interpolating decompositions of
X
over
Y
into isomorphism classes of soft sheaf representations of
X_{*}
over
Y^{\uparrow}
(cf.
Remark

6.34
).
This allows us to construct soft sheaf representations for all objects of
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
:

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
X
be a compact ordered space. Then
X_{*}
admits a soft sheaf representation over the stably compact space
X^{\uparrow}
induced by the interpolating decomposition
X\to X^{\downarrow}
given by the identity function.

Of course, the category
\mathsf{CompOrd}^{\mathrm{op}}
can be replaced with any equivalent category
\mathsf{D}
(e.g., following
[
1
]
, with an appropriate variety of infinitary algebras; cf. also
[
27
]
), thus obtaining soft sheaf representations of objects of
\mathsf{D}
.

7.2.
Commutative Gelfand rings

In this subsection, we shall assume that the reader is familiar with basic notions of point-free topology, see e.g.
[
40
]
.

Let
\mathsf{CRing}
be the category of commutative rings with unit and ring homomorphisms preserving the unit, and fix an arbitrary
A\in\mathsf{CRing}
.
Denote by
X_{A}
the Zariski spectrum of
A
(that is,
X_{A}
is the set of prime ideals of
A
equipped with the Zariski or
hull-kernel
topology). A classical result by Grothendieck
[
26
]
states that
A
is isomorphic to the ring of global sections of a sheaf
F\colon\Omega(X_{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{CRing}
whose stalks are local rings. In fact, the stalk of
F
at
\mathfrak{p}\in X_{A}
is isomorphic to the localization
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
of
A
at
\mathfrak{p}
. Note that this is
not
a soft sheaf representation because the stalks of
F
are not quotients of
A
(cf.
Remark

6.26
). In more detail, for all
\mathfrak{p}\in X_{A}
, the set

k_{\mathfrak{p}}\coloneqq\{U\in\Omega(X_{A})\mid\mathfrak{p}\in U\}

is a filter on
\Omega(X_{A})
and is Scott-open because
X_{A}
is locally compact. Thus, the canonical colimit arrow
F(X_{A})\to\colim_{U\in k_{\mathfrak{p}}}{F(U)}
into the stalk of
F
at
\mathfrak{p}
can be identified with the localization map

A\to A_{\mathfrak{p}}.

The latter is an epimorphism but, in general, fails to be surjective (i.e., a regular epimorphism in the category
\mathsf{CRing}
).

Remark 7.7
.

On the other hand, the
Pierce representation
of a commutative ring
A

is
a soft sheaf representation. In fact, it is induced by the monotone map

\ID{E(A)}\to\ID{A},

where
E(A)
is the Boolean ring of idempotent elements of
A
, that sends an ideal
J
of
E(A)
to the ideal of
A
generated by
J
. See e.g.
[
28
, §V.2]
. In this case,
\ID{E(A)}
can be identified with the frame of opens of the Boolean (i.e., compact, Hausdorff and zero-dimensional) space corresponding to
E(A)
under Stone duality for Boolean algebras
[
44
]
. More generally, if
\mathsf{V}
is a variety of finitary algebras whose signature contains a constant symbol,
every
sheaf of
\mathsf{V}
-algebras over a Boolean space is soft, cf.
[
41
, Lemma 3.3]
.

However, Grothendieck’s sheaf representation induces a soft sheaf representation for a smaller class of commutative rings as we shall now explain. For any
A\in\mathsf{CRing}
, the frame
\RID{A}
of
radical
ideals
9
9

9

An ideal is
radical
if it is the intersection of all prime ideals in which it is contained.
of
A
(ordered by inclusion) is compact and
coherent
, i.e. the subset of
\RID{A}
consisting of the compact elements forms a join-dense sublattice.
Assuming the Prime Ideal Theorem, it can be proved that
\RID{A}
is a spatial frame isomorphic to
\Omega(X_{A})
. This observation was exploited by Banaschewski
[
4
]
to give a point-free version of Grothendieck’s sheaf representation of
A
, replacing the spatial frame
\Omega(X_{A})
with
\RID{A}
.

Now, recall that a
(commutative) Gelfand ring
is a (commutative) ring with unit satisfying the following condition:

\forall x,y.\,(x+y=1\ \Longrightarrow\ \exists a,b.\,(1+xa)(1+yb)=1).

Again assuming the Prime Ideal Theorem, commutative Gelfand rings are exactly the commutative rings with unit in which every prime ideal is contained in a unique maximal ideal.
Even in the absence of the Prime Ideal Theorem, we have that a commutative ring
A
with unit is a commutative Gelfand ring precisely when the frame
\RID{A}
is normal
[
4
, Proposition 1]
(recall that a frame
L
is
normal
if, for all
g,h\in L
such that
g\lor h=\top
, there are
u,v\in L
such that
u\lor g=v\lor h=\top
and
u\wedge v=\bot
). In that case,
\RID{A}
retracts onto its compact regular subframe
\JRID{A}
consisting of the
Jacobson radical ideals
, i.e. those ideals
J
such that, for every
a\in A
, if
1+ra
is invertible modulo
J
for all
r\in A
, then
a\in J
. See
[
4
, Lemma 1 and p. 27]
. Since
\JRID{A}
is a compact regular frame, in view of the following remark there is an isomorphism
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(\JRID{A})\cong\JRID{A}
.

Remark 7.8
.

If
L
is a compact regular frame, then
\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)\cong L
. An explicit isomorphism is given by

L\to\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L),\ \ x\mapsto\{y\in L\mid x\vee y=1\},

whose inverse sends
k\in\sigma\mathrm{Filt}(L)
to
\bigvee\{x\in L\mid\exists y\in k.\ x\land y=0\}
.
In the particular case of spatial compact regular frames (recall that, assuming the Axiom of Choice, every compact regular frame is spatial, see e.g.
[
28
, Proposition III.1.10]
), this reduces to the observation that a compact Hausdorff topology coincides with its
patch topology
.

The category
\mathsf{CRing}
is a well-powered, bicomplete Barr-exact category in which directed colimits commute with finite limits.
Hence, by
Corollary

6.32
and
Remark

6.33
, there is an order isomorphism of small posets

\mathsf{N}\cong\llbracket\mathrm{s\mathchar 45\relax Sh}^{A}_{\Omega}(\JRID{A},\mathsf{CRing})\rrbracket

where
\mathsf{N}
consists of the maps
\JRID{A}\to\ID{A}
preserving finite infima and arbitrary suprema (just observe that any two congruences on
A
commute). Because
\mathsf{CRing}
has no proper subterminal objects, it follows from
Remark

6.15
that
\mathsf{CRing}
-valued (soft)
\Omega
-sheaves coincide with ordinary (soft) sheaves. Therefore the inclusion

\JRID{A}\hookrightarrow\ID{A},

which preserves finite infima and arbitrary suprema because so does
\RID{A}\hookrightarrow\ID{A}
, induces a soft sheaf representation of the commutative Gelfand ring
A
over the compact regular frame
\JRID{A}
. This sheaf representation was first obtained by Banaschewski and Vermeulen
[
5
]
, improving on results of Mulvey
[
38
]
and
Banaschewski
[
3
,
4
]
.

If the Prime Ideal Theorem is assumed, for any commutative Gelfand ring
A
the frame
\JRID{A}
can be identified with
\Omega(\Max{A})
, where
\Max{A}
is the subspace of
X_{A}
consisting of the maximal ideals of
A
(i.e., the closed points of
X_{A}
). The space
\Max{A}
is compact and Hausdorff, and in view of the previous paragraph
A
is isomorphic to the ring of global sections of a soft sheaf
F\colon\Omega(\Max{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{CRing}
(this sheaf representation can also be derived as a special case of
[
21
, Corollary 3.11]
, see p. 2178 in
op. cit.
).

Note that, in contrast with the case of arbitrary commutative rings, for Gelfand rings we get a
soft
sheaf representation. In fact, the stalk of
F
at a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\in\Max{A}
is isomorphic to the quotient ring
A/O_{\mathfrak{m}}
, where the ideal
O_{\mathfrak{m}}
is defined by

O_{\mathfrak{m}}\coloneqq\{a\in A\mid\exists b\in A\setminus\mathfrak{m}\ \text{such that}\ ab=0\}.

See e.g.
[
28
, Lemma V.3.8]
. The unique maximal ideal of
A
containing
O_{\mathfrak{m}}
is
\mathfrak{m}
, hence
A/O_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a local ring (equivalently, note that
A/O_{\mathfrak{m}}\cong A_{\mathfrak{m}}
). The canonical colimit arrow
F(\Max{A})\to\colim_{x\in U}{F(U)}
can then be identified with the quotient map
A\to A/O_{\mathfrak{m}}
, which is a regular epimorphism. This shows that every local section over a point of
\Max{A}
can be extended to a global section. A similar argument shows that every local section defined on a closed subset of
\Max{A}
can be extended to a global section, i.e.
F
is soft.
